This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the **copyright** <note>The legal right that grants the creator of a work exclusive control over its use and distribution for a set period of time.</note> to expire and the book to enter the **public domain** <term>public domain: a status belonging to creative works that are no longer protected by intellectual property laws, allowing the public to use them freely.</term>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other **marginalia** <term>marginalia: notes, sketches, or comments written by hand in the margins of a book.</term> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

### Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on **automated querying** <term>automated querying: the use of software scripts or bots to send a high volume of search requests to a system.</term>.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on **machine translation** <note>The use of computer software to translate text from one language to another.</note>, **optical character recognition (OCR)** <term>optical character recognition: the technology that converts images of text into machine-readable and searchable data.</term>, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. **Copyright infringement** <term>copyright infringement: the unauthorized use of works protected by copyright law in a way that violates the owner's exclusive rights.</term> liability can be quite severe.

### About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

# About this book

This is a digital copy of a work that has been preserved for generations on library shelves before being carefully digitized by Google as part of a project aimed at allowing internet users to discover the world's entire literary heritage online.

Since this book is relatively old, it is no longer protected by **copyright** <term>copyright: a legal right that grants the creator of a work exclusive control over its use and distribution for a specific period of time</term> and now belongs to the **public domain** <term>public domain: the state of belonging to the public as a whole, not subject to copyright</term>. The phrase "belonging to the public domain" means that the book in question has never been subject to copyright or that its legal rights have expired. The requirements for a book to fall into the public domain can vary from one country to another. Books free of copyright are so many links to the past. They are witnesses to the richness of our history, our cultural heritage, and human knowledge, and are too often difficult for the public to access.

Footnotes and other annotations in the margins of the text present in the original volume are included in this file as a memory of the long journey traveled by the work from the publishing house, through the library, to finally end up in your hands.

# Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize works belonging to the public domain and thus make them accessible to everyone. These books are indeed the property of all, and we are simply the guardians of this heritage. This is, however, an expensive project. Consequently, and with a view to continuing the dissemination of these inexhaustible resources, we have taken the necessary steps to prevent potential abuse by third-party commercial sites, notably by establishing technical constraints regarding **automated queries** <term>automated queries: the use of software scripts or "bots" to send large volumes of search requests to a system</term>.

We also ask that you:

+ *Do not use the files for commercial purposes* We designed the Google Book Search program for use by individuals. We therefore ask that you use these files only for personal purposes. They cannot, in fact, be employed for any commercial goal whatsoever.

+ *Do not perform automated queries* Do not send any automated query of any kind to the Google system. If you are conducting research concerning translation software, optical character recognition, or any other field requiring access to large quantities of text, please do not hesitate to contact us. For the realization of this type of work, we encourage the use of works and documents belonging to the public domain and would be happy to be of use to you.

+ *Do not remove the attribution* The Google watermark contained in each file is essential for informing internet users of our project and allowing them to access more documents through the Google Book Search Program. Do not remove it under any circumstances.

+ *Stay within the law* Whatever use you plan to make of the files, remember that it is your responsibility to ensure you respect the law. If a work belongs to the American public domain, do not assume that the same applies in other countries. The legal duration of a book's copyright varies from one country to another. We are therefore not in a position to list the works whose use is authorized and those whose use is not. Do not believe that the simple fact of displaying a book on Google Book Search means that it can be used in any way whatsoever throughout the entire world. The penalty to which you might expose yourself in case of copyright violation can be severe.

# About the Google Book Search service

By promoting the search for and access to an increasing number of books available in many languages, including French, Google wishes to contribute to promoting cultural diversity through Google Book Search. Indeed, the Google Book Search Program allows internet users to discover the world's literary heritage while helping authors and publishers reach a wider audience. You can perform online searches in the full text of this work at the address <insert>http://books.google.com</insert>

***The Baroulkos or The Mechanics of Heron of Alexandria*** <note>The title "Baroulkos" comes from the Greek words for "weight" and "to pull." Heron was a 1st-century AD inventor often called "the Leonardo da Vinci of antiquity."</note>, is one of the most important treatises that antiquity has left us in the branch of mathematical sciences to which it belongs. Because of its great length and the fame of its author, it is one of the works that can best inform us about the character and development of <term>ancient mechanics</term> <note>In this context, mechanics refers to the study of the five simple machines—the lever, pulley, wheel and axle, wedge, and screw—used to move heavy loads.</note>. The Greek original of this work is currently lost; however, an Arabic version exists—which is more or less faithful to the original—held in the Library of Leiden ¹. It is this text that we are presenting here.

¹ 983, Code 51 (1) Golius. <note>original: "DCCCCLXXXIII, Cod. 51 (1) Gol." This refers to the manuscript collection of Jacobus Golius (1596–1667), a Dutch orientalist who brought many scientific manuscripts from the Middle East to Leiden University.</note>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion regarding the historical dating of Heron of Alexandria. While the previous page established the importance of the Arabic version of the "Mechanics," this section delves into the scholarly debate over whether Heron lived in the 3rd century BCE or after the beginning of the Christian era, based on linguistic clues and historical citations.</meta>

[The traditional view of Heron's date] ought to be overturned¹. The attribution *Heron [of] Ctesibius* <note>original: "Ἥρωνος Κτησιβίου". Ctesibius was a famous inventor of the 3rd century BCE; this phrase usually suggests Heron was his student.</note>, it has been argued, is a double attribution implying the disjunctive *or*. The *Belopoeica* <note>A treatise on the construction of war engines and projectiles.</note> should therefore be attributed to Heron or to Ctesibius, and not to Heron as a disciple of Ctesibius; the note by Athenaeus <note>Athenaeus Mechanicus, a writer on siege engines.</note> would be of no value, as its author lived at a much later period. Other considerations tend to push Heron’s era until after the beginning of the Christian era: the presence of Latinisms in his works, such as the words *ground* <note>original: "ἔδαφος" (Latin *passus*, meaning a pace or step)</note> and *miles* <note>original: "μιλία" (Latin *millia*)</note> in the last chapter of the treatise *On the Dioptra*² <note>The dioptra was a sophisticated surveying instrument used for measuring distances and angles.</note> and the words *boiler* <note>original: "μιλιάριον" (Latin *milliarium*)</note> and *as* <note>original: "ἀσσάριον" (Latin *assis*, a small Roman coin)</note> in the *Pneumatics*³; also the fact that Heron is not cited by any author before the 2nd century of our era, and that specifically his name does not appear in the works of Vitruvius <note>A famous Roman architect and engineer of the 1st century BCE who wrote "De Architectura."</note>. Th.-H. Martin⁴ was already aware of the Latinisms encountered in Heron's writings; however, admitting the possibility of loanwords taken from Latin by the Alexandrians during the time of Ptolemy Euergetes II <note>A ruler of Egypt in the 2nd century BCE.</note>, he did not see this as an objection against the accepted opinion. Today, the hypothesis of this borrowing—acknowledged by Heron himself and implying that certain Greek terms had fallen into disuse—seems...

³ *Ancient Mathematicians*, Paris, 1693, p. 165, 180 and 224.
⁴ *Research on the life and works of Heron of Alexandria*, vol. IV, series I of the *Memoirs presented by various scholars to the Academy of Inscriptions*, 1854, p. 26.

of Saint Mark in Venice <note>The Biblioteca Nazionale Marciana.</note> regarding a Greek manuscript of Heron’s *Mechanics* <note>original: "Heronis Mechanica"</note>. He also noted, in Miller’s catalogue of the Greek manuscripts of the Escorial¹ <note>A historic royal monastery and library in Spain.</note>, the title *Mechanical Introductions* <note>original Greek: "Εἰσαγωγαὶ Μηχανικαί"</note> by Heron. Although it was difficult to believe that a work such as the *Mechanics*—a loss so often lamented by scholars—could remain unnoticed in one of the great libraries of Europe, we felt compelled to verify this information. Martin had been unable to do so, as bibliographic research was not as easy in his time as it can be in our own.

We had the opportunity to visit Rome and Venice ourselves. In Venice, Montfaucon’s <note>Bernard de Montfaucon (1655–1741), a Benedictine monk and a founding father of paleography.</note> error is evident. The manuscript he cites, without having seen it himself, no doubt, actually exists in the National Library of Saint Mark, and it bears the number CCLXIII in the catalogue by *Zannetti*. It matches Montfaucon’s descriptions so closely as to leave no uncertainty regarding this identification. The agreement fails on only one point: what the author of the *Library of Libraries* <note>original Latin: "Bibliotheca bibliothecarum," a major reference work by Montfaucon.</note> calls the *Mechanics* of Heron is, in fact, the *Pneumatics*.

In Rome, our research did not lead to such a clear result. We had to find a manuscript that had been part of the *Slusiana* Library, and another that had belonged to Cardinal *Sirlet*, all

The title of the manuscript informs us that he undertook its translation by order of <term>Abû’l’abbâs Ahmed ibn Almu’tasim</term>; this is the caliph <term>Al-Musta'in Billah</term>, a descendant of <term>Harun</term> <note>Harun al-Rashid (r. 786–809), the famous Abbasid caliph.</note>. This title adds that the work was translated directly from Greek into Arabic¹.

We must now seek out all the primary links by which our text connects to the already published works of <term>Heron</term> and to other works of Roman and Greek antiquity. First of all, are there any known fragments of the <term>Mechanics</term>? Several are known. The first², which is in fact only a fragment within the *Mechanics* itself, forms the first chapter. We have already spoken of it twice, having recalled that it was published by <term>Brugmans</term> based on the Latin version by <term>Golius</term>, and that it was found at the end of the treatise <term>On the Dioptra</term> <note>original Greek: Περὶ διόπτρας (Peri dioptras). A dioptra was a classical surveying instrument.</note>. This fragment is furthermore cited by <term>Pappus</term>³; however, Pappus does not reproduce it, and he proposes the same problem treated therein while using a different ratio of wheels to pinions and a different ratio of motive power to weight. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Heron of Alexandria’s mathematical and mechanical works. The first word completes "proportionnelles" (proportional) from the previous page, finishing a sentence about finding proportional lines—a key step in solving ancient geometric problems.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>

[pro]portional means between two given lines.

The solution offered by <term>Heron</term> in the first book of his *Mechanics* is repeated by him in the *Belopoeeca* <note>original title: *Belopœeca*. A Greek treatise on the construction of "artillery" or missile-throwing engines, such as catapults and ballistae.</note>¹. There, it is modified by this question: to construct a machine three times the size of one having a certain line αβ as its diameter. In the *Mechanics*, it forms part of a series of propositions on similarity that we have gathered into a single section. We can therefore assume that it has its rightful place in both works.

The Delian problem <note>A famous ancient puzzle also known as "doubling the cube." According to legend, the citizens of Delos were told by an oracle to double the size of their altar to stop a plague.</note> was famous in antiquity; Heron’s solution appears to have been greatly admired. <term>Pappus</term>² reproduces it alongside the solutions of <term>Eratosthenes</term>, <term>Nicomedes</term>, and his own. He attributes the citation to both of Heron’s treatises: "as Heron teaches in the *Mechanics* and in the *Catapult-making*" <note>original Latin: *ut Hero docet in mechanicis et in catapulticis*</note>. <term>Eutocius</term>³ also repeats it in his commentary on <term>Archimedes</term>’ *On the Sphere and Cylinder*, and he compares it to the solutions provided by other authors. He attributes it to the *Mechanical Introductions* and to the *Belopoeeca*: "Heron in the *Mechanical Introductions* and in the *Artillery-making*" <note>original Greek: εἰς Ἥρων ἐν μηχανικαῖς εἰσαγωγαῖς καὶ ἐν τοῖς βελοποιικοῖς (eis Hērōn en mēchanikais eisagōgais kai en tois belopoiikois)</note>. Clearly, these *Mechanical Introductions* are not distinct from the *Mechanics* itself. We have

² Book III, proposition 4, p. 57, and Book III, proposition 5, p. 63-65. <meta>This page continues the investigation into the titles and citations of Heron of Alexandria’s works, specifically confirming the title "Mechanical Introductions" through Arabic manuscripts and comparing Heron’s mechanical designs with those recorded by the mathematician Pappus of Alexandria.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

encountered the same title in the *Catalogue of Greek Manuscripts of the Escurial* <note>The Escurial (El Escorial) is a historic royal monastery in Spain known for its vast library of ancient manuscripts.</note> by Miller. We find it again—and this is more interesting—in our Arabic text, at the end of the first book: **"As for the beginning of the discourse on the Introductions to the Art of Mechanical Devices, this suffices as the first discourse"** <note>original Arabic: اما فى اوّل القول من مداخل صناعة الحيل فيكفى بهذا; original French translation: "cela suffit comme premier discours des Introductions mécaniques."</note>. The citation from <term>Eutocius</term> <note>Eutocius of Ascalon (c. 480 – c. 540 AD) was a scholar who wrote influential commentaries on the works of Archimedes.</note> is thus perfectly justified.

Let us resume the list of citations from <term>Pappus</term> <note>Pappus of Alexandria (c. 290 – c. 350 AD) was one of the last great Greek mathematicians of antiquity; his "Collection" (Synagoge) preserves much of what we know about ancient mechanics.</note> collected in his eighth book. After explaining in his own way—but without departing much from that of <term>Heron</term>—the construction of a train of gears, Pappus¹ moves the wheel farthest from the weight using a screw meshing with it. Heron, he adds, explained in the <term>Mechanics</term> how this should be done. Indeed, Heron, who does not apply this mode of transmission in his descriptions of the gear train, explains it at length in another part of our treatise². Further on, Pappus³ shows how each turn of the screw moves one tooth of the wheel meshed with it; this is what Heron, he says, demonstrated in the <term>Mechanics</term>. We also find this demonstration in our book⁴. The construction of the screw explained by Pappus⁵ is very similar to that given by Heron⁶; but Pappus

<page-num>17</page-num>

does not cite him; he states that he is referring back to **Apollonius of Perge.** <note>Apollonius of Perge (c. 240–190 BC) was a Greek geometer famous for his work on conic sections.</note>

The proposition that large circles prevail over small ones, if they rotate around the same center, is given by **Pappus**¹ as having been demonstrated by **Archimedes** in his treatise *On Balances* <note>original Greek: *Περὶ ζυγῶν* (Peri zygon)</note> and by **Philo** <note>Philo of Byzantium (c. 280–220 BC), a Greek engineer and writer on mechanics.</note> and **Hero** in their *Mechanics*. This proposition is actually found in several passages of our book². The law of equilibrium between the power and the weight acting on the <term>windlass</term> <note>A windlass is a machine used for moving or lifting heavy weights using a rope or chain wound around a horizontal cylinder or drum.</note> is also, according to **Pappus**³, demonstrated in **Hero’s** *Mechanics*. It is, indeed, presented and explained there on several occasions and in several forms⁴.

After the part of Book VIII where the citations we have reported are found, there follows in **Pappus** a fairly long series of excerpts taken from **Hero’s** *Mechanics*. This part⁵ begins with these words: “So much, then, concerning the <term>barulkos</term>,” <note>original Greek: *Τοσαῦτα μὲν οὖν περὶ τοῦ βαρουλκοῦ*; original Latin translation provided in the text: *hæc igitur de barulco*. The *barulkos* was a specialized "weight-lifter" machine using gears.</note> which may not refer to all the passages noted by **Pappus**, but only to the one where he describes the gear train; they would thus mean: “that is what concerns the hoist”; immediately afterward the author adds “but of those five powers we mentioned <note>the five simple machines: the lever, pulley, wheel and axle, wedge, and screw</note> we shall extract a shorter explanation . . . . .” <note>original Latin: *sed earum quas diximus quinque potentiarum... breviorem expositionem... excerpemus*</note>

<meta>This page continues the geometric analysis of circular motion from the previous page (numbered 43 in the sequence). The text completes a sentence describing the relative directions of movement—right and left—before transitioning from the study of equal circles to the study of unequal circles.</meta>

right, and the other toward the left. It can also be said that the movements occur in the same direction when one considers points taken at equal distances from ζ <note>the Greek letter zeta</note> and χ <note>the Greek letter chi</note>, or points taken on the two arcs ζγ and ηδ at equal distances from γ and δ; the same remark should be made for the two arcs γθ and δχ, and for the two arcs θε and χε, and to regard the movements on these arcs as being in the same directions. We say, for example, that the two points δ and ε move in the same direction—point ε being mobile on the arc εζγ, and point δ on the arc δχε—insofar as they respectively approach or move away from points ζ and χ by equal amounts. Now, this movement can also be called movement in opposite directions. This is why the opposition or identity of the senses of movement are relative; it is therefore necessary to distinguish in each movement those movements that occur in the same sense and those that occur in opposite senses¹.

This is what should be considered in the case where the circles are equal. What follows concerns unequal circles:

->Fig. 3.<-

<image-desc>A geometric diagram labeled Fig. 3 showing two tangent circles of unequal size. The larger circle on the left has its center at α (alpha), with its horizontal diameter defined by points γ (gamma) on the left and ε (epsilon) on the right. Points ζ (zeta) and θ (theta) are located at the top and bottom of this circle, respectively. *εζγ* <note>epsilon-zeta-gamma</note> will then be double the arc *εηδ* <note>epsilon-eta-delta</note>, as <term>Archimedes</term> <note>Archimedes of Syracuse (c. 287–212 BCE), the preeminent Greek mathematician who defined the relationships between the parts of a circle</note> has demonstrated; therefore, in the time it takes point *ε* to traverse the arc *εζ*, moving in the direction of *γ*, this same point *ε* moving in the reverse direction traverses the arc *εηδ*; also in the time that this point starting from *ζ* takes to describe the arc *ζγ*, a point starting from *δ* describes the arc *δκ* and arrives at point *ε*. Therefore, the point describing the circumference *εηδκε* sometimes follows a direction opposite to that of the point describing the arc *εζγ* and sometimes a direction in the same sense. Likewise, during the time that point *γ* traverses the arc *γεθ*, point *ε* traverses the circle *εηδκε*, sometimes going in the direction of *γ* and sometimes in the opposite direction.

[quadri]lateral <note>The word "latère" is the second half of the French word "quadrilatère," or quadrilateral.</note> $\alpha\beta\gamma\delta$ <note>alpha-beta-gamma-delta</note> has parallel sides and right angles. Therefore, the four lines $\delta\eta$, $\eta\alpha$, $\eta\beta$, and $\eta\gamma$ <note>segments connecting the center point eta to the four corners</note> are equal. The line $\eta\delta$ being equal to the line $\eta\alpha$, and the line $\eta\zeta$ <note>eta-zeta</note> having already been drawn, we have: $\delta\zeta \times \zeta\alpha + \overline{\alpha\eta}^2 = \overline{\eta\zeta}^2$ <note>This formula expresses a geometric property where the product of two segments of a line plus the square of the distance to the midpoint equals the square of the total distance to the center</note>; and likewise: $\delta\epsilon \times \epsilon\gamma + \overline{\gamma\eta}^2 = \overline{\eta\epsilon}^2$. ->Fig. 6.<-

<image-desc>A geometric diagram labeled "Fig. 6." It depicts a rectangle $\delta\alpha\beta\gamma$ where $\delta$ is the bottom-left corner, $\alpha$ is the bottom-right corner, $\beta$ is the top-right corner, and $\gamma$ is the top-left corner. $\epsilon\eta$ and $\eta\zeta$ are equal; it follows that: $\delta\zeta \times \zeta\alpha + \overline{\alpha\eta}^2 = \delta\epsilon \times \epsilon\gamma + \overline{\gamma\eta}^2$. But $\overline{\gamma\eta}^2 = \overline{\alpha\eta}^2$; there remains $\delta\epsilon \times \epsilon\gamma = \delta\zeta \times \zeta\alpha$ or rather $\frac{\epsilon\delta}{\delta\zeta} = \frac{\zeta\alpha}{\gamma\epsilon}$; moreover, we have: $\frac{\epsilon\delta}{\delta\zeta} = \frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha\zeta} = \frac{\epsilon\gamma}{\gamma\beta}$. Therefore the ratios $\frac{\alpha\zeta}{\gamma\epsilon}$ and $\frac{\gamma\epsilon}{\beta\gamma}$ are equal to the ratio $\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha\zeta}$. Thus we have constructed two consecutive **mean proportionals** <term>mean proportionals: in a sequence of three or more values, the intermediate values that maintain a constant ratio between the start and the end (e.g., in a:x = x:y = y:b, x and y are mean proportionals)</term> between $\alpha\beta$ and $\beta\gamma$, namely the two lines $\alpha\zeta$ and $\gamma\epsilon$. This is what we intended to demonstrate. <note>original: "C'est ce que nous voulions démontrer," the French equivalent of the Latin "Quod Erat Demonstrandum" (Q.E.D.).</note>

Now, half of αε plus that of ηβ, plus those of αη and of εβ, equals the whole of αβ, and this is what rests upon the set of columns.

If the columns are more numerous, the same reasoning reveals what portion of the weight each of them supports: it is likewise the same.

This book serves as a sufficient first introduction to the <term>mechanical arts</term> <note>A historical term for the study of engineering and the practical application of machines.</note>. In what follows, we shall discuss the <term>five simple machines</term> <note>The "simple machines" are the foundational components of mechanical devices, traditionally categorized as the lever, the wheel and axle, the pulley, the inclined plane, and the screw.</note> with which one moves or pulls <term>heavy bodies</term> <note>original: "corps graves"; in early physics, this refers to objects with mass that are naturally inclined to move toward the center of the Earth due to gravity.</note>, as well as the <term>physical causes</term> that make them work; we shall also treat other matters that are of the greatest utility regarding the transport and lifting of <term>heavy bodies</term>.

...the other end; the weight seemed lighter to them. They placed under this piece of wood a stone whose name is <term>hypomochlium</term> <note>From the Greek *hypomochlion*; this is the **fulcrum**, the pivot point on which a lever turns.</note> <gloss>1</gloss>, which means: "placed under the lever"; and pressing down again, they found the weight even lighter still. Once this force was demonstrated, it was understood that it was possible to move considerable weights by this means. This piece of wood is called a **lever**, whether it is round or squared; and the closer the stone placed under it is brought to the weight being moved, the easier the movement becomes, as we shall demonstrate in the following section.

### 3. Third simple machine.

The third simple machine is the one called the <term>moufle</term> <note>A system of pulleys, known today as a **block and tackle**.</note>. When we wish to raise a weight, whatever it may be, we attach ropes to it, and we intend to pull the ropes until it is lifted; for this, we require a force equal to the weight we wish to raise. If, after detaching the ropes from the weight, we tie one of their ends to a fixed beam, and then pass the other end under a **pulley** <note>original: *poulie*; a wheel with a grooved rim for a rope.</note> secured to the middle of the load, and then pull the ropes, we will move the weight more easily. If we hook another pulley to the fixed beam and pass the end of the rope through it, pulling it tight, we will move the weight with even greater ease. If we attach yet another pulley to the load to thread the end of the rope through,

multiply the pulleys through which the ropes pass, the greater this ease will be. It is proper that the first end of the rope be fixed, attached to the stationary beam, and that the rope goes from there toward the weight. The pulleys that are hung from the fixed support must be secured by means of another piece of wood, and they must turn around a single axis which is called a <term>manganum</term> <note>original: *manganum*; from the Greek *manganon* (μαγγανον), referring to a hoisting machine or the block that houses multiple pulleys</note>¹; this piece of wood is attached to the fixed beam by other ropes. As for the pulleys linked to the load <note>original: *fardeau*</note>, they are arranged on another axis equal to the first and attached to the load. It is necessary that the pulleys be mounted on the axis in such a way that they cannot come too close to one another because, if they touched, their rotation would become difficult. We have said that the more the pulleys were multiplied, the greater was the ease with which the weight was raised, and that the end of the rope must be attached to the support; we shall prove this in the following section.

### 4. Fourth simple machine.

— The fourth simple machine, following the previous ones, is that which is called the **wedge** <note>original: *coin*</note>. It serves in some of the preparations of perfumers <note>likely referring to the use of presses to extract oils</note> and to produce the adhesion of separated parts in certain woodworking projects. Its uses are varied; but most often, it is used to split the lower part of stones that one wishes to detach, after having first separated them on the sides from the

<margin>¹ Greek *manganon* <note>original: *μαγγανον*</note>.</margin>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the block and tackle by explaining how pulleys should be mounted on an axis (manganum) and introduces the wedge as the fourth simple machine used in masonry and carpentry.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the wedge, highlighting its unique ability to split material through its own internal pressure after being struck. ->—•—( 101 )—•—<-

mass from which one wishes to detach them. For this purpose, one could not use any of the other machines, even by combining them all; the <term>wedge</term> <note>original: *coin*</note> alone can act in this case. It acts by the blow that strikes it, whatever that blow may be, and it does not stop acting after the blow is delivered. This is evident; indeed, often, without the <term>wedge</term> being struck, it makes noise and moves, because it splits by its own force. The sharper the angle of the <term>wedge</term>, the more easily it acts, as we shall demonstrate.

## 5. Fifth simple machine. — It is the one called the <term>screw</term> <note>original: *vis*</note>.

The instruments of which we have spoken rest on very clear principles and are complete in themselves; this is observed in the many circumstances where they are employed. The <term>screw</term>, on the contrary, presents some difficulty in its construction and in its use, whether it is used alone or another power is associated with it. It is, however, nothing other than a <term>curved wedge</term> <note>original: *coin courbe*</note>, which does not receive blows, but which a lever sets in motion. What we are about to say will make this proposition evident.

We thus define the nature of the line traced on the <term>screw</term>: let us suppose that one of the <term>edges</term> <note>original: *arêtes*; here referring to a vertical generating line on the side of a cylinder</note> of a <term>cylindrical</term> figure moves across the surface of the <term>cylinder</term>, and that a point is mobile on this <term>edge</term> starting from its extremity; this point travels the entire <term>edge</term> in the time it takes the <term>edge</term> to make one full rotation around the <term>cylindrical</term> surface and return to the position from which it started. The <term>curve</term> <note>this describes a helix, the mathematical basis for screw threads</note> described by the

The <term>helical groove</term> found on the cylinder is sometimes square and sometimes lenticular <note>Lens-shaped or V-shaped with curved sides.</note>. The square groove is that whose walls are perpendicular to the surface of the cylinder and which is bounded by two lines; the lenticular groove is that which has inclined walls and is bounded by a single line. This latter is called a <term>lenticular thread</term> and the other is called a <term>square thread</term>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the worm gear mechanism from page 124. It focuses on the physical interaction between the screw's helical thread and the "tylos" (the tooth or peg of the gear wheel), explaining the conditions necessary for the machine to hold a load without slipping backward.</meta>

( 125 )

the <term>tylos</term> <note>original: "tylos"; a Greek term for a peg, pin, or the tooth of a gear wheel.</note> moves, according to what we have said above, and lifts the weight as it rises. It is necessary, when the <term>screw</term> is not turning, that this <term>wooden finger</term> <note>original: "doigt de bois"; refers to the individual tooth of the drum that engages with the screw.</note> be kept at rest in its place, regardless of the force applied to it. This ensures that the moment one stops turning the handle, the weight does not overcome the <term>screw</term>; that is to say, this <term>finger</term>, having been inserted into the <term>helical groove</term> and serving as a stop, must not slip out of this <term>groove</term>. If it were to slip, the entire weight would fall back down to the place from which it was hoisted.

This component does not exit the <term>helical groove</term> when its end is well-fitted to the <term>groove</term> and enters it as if into a shoe. For this to happen, the turns of the <term>screw</term> must be nearly parallel to the base of the <term>cylinder</term> on which the <term>screw</term> is carved <note>This refers to a "low pitch" screw, where the threads are tightly packed and nearly horizontal.</note>. When the turns are arranged in this way, they act like shoes gripping the wooden component that pulls the weight.

If, on the contrary, the turns of the <term>screw</term> formed by the <term>helical groove</term> have a very steep inclination—to the point of being nearly parallel to the vertical edge of the <term>cylinder</term>—then, when a very heavy weight is suspended from the <term>wooden finger</term>, or when a considerable force presses upon it, this component will react against the rotation intended for the <term>screw</term> and will cause it to spin in the opposite direction. 

By this, we see that the <term>screw</term> can move the component called the <term>tylos</term>, just as the <term>screw</term> itself can be moved by that component. The screw moves the peg when the <term>helical groove</term> is close to the shape of a circle <note>Meaning the thread is closer to a horizontal ring than a vertical line.</note>; in this case, when the rotation of the screw stops, the <term>wooden finger</term> remains at rest in the same place, held—

<meta>This page continues the technical description of mechanical advantage. -> ( 129 ) <-

...first bring the <term>power</term> <note>original: "puissance"; in this context, it refers to the input force or effort applied to the machine.</note> to a state of equilibrium with the weight; because, once this equilibrium is achieved, it will be possible for us to ensure that the <term>power</term> overcomes the weight by adding a slight excess to the ratio between the components of the instrument. The <term>axle</term> <note>original: "arbre"; literally "tree," commonly used for a shaft or spindle in machinery.</note> around which the rope carrying the weight is wound is at **α**; the <term>drum</term> <note>original: "tambour"; a large wheel or gear mounted on the axle.</note> mounted on the axle is at point **β**. To facilitate the construction of the instrument, let us make the diameter of the <term>drum</term> equal to only five times the diameter of the <term>axle</term>. It is then necessary that the <term>power</term> applied to the <term>drum</term>, which must balance a weight of 1,000 <term>talents</term> <note>A "talent" was an ancient unit of weight, roughly 26 to 33 kilograms depending on the region.</note>, be 200 talents; but the <term>power</term> available to us is only 5 talents. It is therefore not possible for us to move the given weight by means of the <term>drum</term> **β** with this <term>power</term>.

**Question 3 :** Why does the same weight added to a scale in equilibrium not always produce the same inclination, and why does it produce a greater inclination when the scale is less heavily loaded? If, for example, there are 3 <term>mines</term> <note>original: "mines"; an ancient Greek unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 430–600 grams.</note> in both pans and we add a half-mine to one of them, this pan tips significantly. If, in each pan, there are 10 mines, and we place an additional half-mine in one of them, the inclination of the <term>beam</term> <note>original: "fléau"; the horizontal bar of the balance scale from which the pans hang.</note> is very slight in this case. — It is because, in these various cases, the weight is moved by different powers; the 3 mines are moved by a weight equal to themselves, plus ⅓ of that weight <note>The text states 1/3, though mathematically a half-mine is 1/6 of three mines. This may be an error in the original manuscript or the 18th/19th-century French translation.</note>, whereas the 10 mines are moved by a weight equal to themselves, plus half of a tenth of that weight; for a half-mine is ¹/₂₀ of 10 mines and ⅓ of 3: now, the weight that is moved by the greater power has an easier motion.

To demonstrate this, let us extend the line $\eta\zeta$ until it reaches point $\gamma$; since the path $\alpha\zeta\eta$ is longer than the straight line $\gamma\zeta\eta$, it is clear that point $\eta$ sits higher than point $\beta$. 

Let us further suppose that the cord is pulled from a point $\varepsilon$ and moved to the line $\gamma\eta$, while the weight remains in the same relative state—that is, it is still at the very end of the total length of the cord $\alpha\beta$. Since the segment $\alpha\varepsilon$ is longer than $\alpha\zeta$, the point $\varepsilon$ will fall lower than $\zeta$, arriving for example at point $\theta$. 

Let us join $\alpha$ and $\theta$; the segment $\alpha\theta$ will now be part of the path $\alpha\theta\eta$. I say that the suspended weight now falls lower than point $\eta$. 

Indeed, since the sum of the lines $\alpha\zeta$ and $\zeta\theta$ is greater than the single line $\alpha\theta$, and the line $\eta\theta$ is shared by both paths, it follows that $\alpha\zeta + \zeta\eta$ (which is equal to the total cord $\alpha\beta$) is greater than $\alpha\theta + \theta\eta$. If we let the sum of $\alpha\theta + \theta\varkappa$ be equal to the total length $\alpha\beta$, the weight will then settle at point $\varkappa$. ### BOOK III.
---
I. --- 1. In the preceding book, we discussed the five <term>simple machines</term> <note>These traditionally include the lever, wheel and axle, pulley, inclined plane, and screw.</note>, and we demonstrated the principles by which heavy weights are supported by small <term>forces</term> <note>original: *puissances*. In this historical context, "power" refers to the input force applied to a machine.</note>. In this regard, we have adhered to the theories of most of our predecessors. We explained why the movement of the force is slower in larger devices; and we presented various propositions used by those who teach mechanics and the laws of gravity, providing sufficient detail for beginners. In this book, we will describe instruments that serve to facilitate the aforementioned operations and which assist in moving heavy bodies. We will also describe devices used for pressing, as their operation also requires the application of great forces.

Loads that are dragged across the ground are carried on the <term>tortoise</term> <note>original: *la tortue*. A "tortoise" in ancient mechanics refers to a solid wooden sled or base used to transport heavy stones or materials.</note>. This is a solid body made from a single piece of wood, squared off and rounded at both ends. Upon this piece, the weights are placed; at its ends, cables or other means of pulling are attached, which are pulled taut to move the tortoise. One pulls the...

...is wider than the second, but they are equal in length, that is to say, the line χμ is equal to εη. We hollow out the stone according to this outline, giving this excavation a depth that is proportional to the weight of the stone. In the section εζηθ, the cavity has its walls exactly perpendicular to the plane of the face; however, in the section χλμν, its walls are oblique, and the cavity is wider at the bottom than at the surface. 

In summary, this cavity has the shape of a type of joint <note>This describes a "dovetail" or flared mortise designed to lock a lifting tool in place.</note> where the narrow part is represented by χλμν and the wide part by εζηθ. Based on this plan, we manufacture an iron component that can fit into the narrow part, to the top of which a ring is welded. This component, first inserted into the cavity εζηθ, simply passes through it; it is then pushed back, turning it slightly until it enters the constricted section, from which it can no longer be removed. 

A piece of wood is then fitted into the section εζηθ to wedge the iron <term>bolt</term> <note>original: *verrou*. Here, it refers to the iron shank of the lifting device.</note> in place; then, through the ring welded to the bolt, we pass the rope that previously supported the collar in which the stone was placed. In this manner, the stone is transported until it reaches the desired location without anything hindering it. Once it is seated in its place, the wooden wedges are removed, the bolt is withdrawn, and the apparatus is adapted to another stone.

— + ( 115 ) + —

...a small <term>wedge</term> <note>original: *asfīn*. A tapered piece used to regulate the depth of the cut.</note>, and we insert it into the channeled groove and strike it until the iron <term>rod</term> <note>original: *qadīb*. This serves as the cutting blade.</note> emerges and sits between the two pieces <note>The manuscript uses the word *al-qit‘atayn* (the two pieces) instead of *qadībatayn* (two small rods).</note>. Once we have done that, we mount the <term>screw</term> <note>original: *al-lawlab*. Here referring to the guide-screw or lead-screw.</note> into a piece of wood in which a hole has been bored, which must be perfectly straight and sized to the thickness of the screw. 

Then, we bore small holes into the sides of this channeled cavity that reach into it, and we install small, slanted, rounded <term>pegs</term> <note>original: *awtād*. These act as followers that travel within the threads of the guide-screw to move the mechanism.</note> inside them <note>The relative pronoun *al-ladhī* (which) is omitted in the original manuscript here.</note>. We push them through until they rest within the threads of the screw. 

Next, we take the piece of wood in which we intend to carve the <term>female screw</term> <note>original: *al-lawlab al-unthā*. The internal threading of a nut or a heavy wooden block.</note> and bore a hole in it matching the size of the screw's shaft. We connect the wood in which we mounted the [guide] screw to this piece [the workpiece] using two upright supports, tightening them as firmly as possible. 

Then we insert the shaft containing the wedge into the hole of the wood we wish to carve as the female screw. We bore <note>The manuscript uses a variant spelling *nasqubu* for the standard *nathqubu* (we bore).</note> a hole in the upper end of the screw to insert pegs [to serve as handles], and we turn it until it penetrates the wood. We continue to turn it <note>The manuscript erroneously reads *nurīduhā* (we want it) instead of *nudīruhā* (we turn it).</note> up and down, and we attend to this wedge by striking it time after time <note>Striking the wedge pushes the cutting rod further out, allowing for a progressively deeper cut with each pass.</note> until it carves the female screw to the depth we desire. In this way, we will have finished carving the female screw. ۞

<summary>This page describes the assembly and operation of a specialized woodworking jig used to carve internal screw threads. It explains how a guide-screw and adjustable iron cutter are used to progressively deepen the grooves inside a wooden block to create a "female" nut.</summary>
<keywords>mechanical engineering, woodworking, screw-making, carpentry tools, internal threading, technical manual</keywords>

<insert>[21]</insert> As for the **female screw** <term>female screw</term> <note>original: *al-lawlab al-unthā*. In pre-modern mechanics, the 'female screw' refers to the internal threading of a nut or a hole, while the 'male' is the bolt.</note>, it is made in the following way:

A piece of hard wood is taken, its length being more than twice the length of the intended female screw, and its thickness equal to it. On one side, across half the length of the wood, a screw thread is carved corresponding to its half. Let the depth of the **screw-grooves** <note>original: *al-dawa’ir al-lawlabiyya*. Literally "screw circles," referring to the helical paths.</note> in it be equal to the depth of the grooves of the screw we wish to turn within this female screw. 

We then turn <note>original: *nakhrutu*. To shape on a lathe.</note> the other half of the wood to the thickness of the base of the screw-grooves until we make it like a **peg** <term>peg</term> <note>original: *watad*. Here, it refers to the smooth, cylindrical portion of the mandrel that will hold the cutter.</note> of uniform thickness. We mark the diameters of the two circular bases of the wood and divide each diameter into three equal parts. At one of the two division marks, we draw a line perpendicular to the diameter. Then, from the two ends of this perpendicular line, we draw two straight lines along the entire length of the peg. 

This is made possible for us if we place this peg on a vertical board and mark it with **calipers** <term>calipers</term> <note>original: *bil-kalbatayn*. A tool used for measurement and marking.</note> <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: *bil-kabis*. The manuscript variant suggests "with the press."</note> until it reaches the screw-carving. Then we work carefully <gloss>2</gloss> <note>original: *wa-sataf*. The manuscript variant is likely a corruption of "to work finely."</note> with a fine saw until we saw through the section following the screw-carving. 

Next, we separate this marked-out third from the peg. In the middle of the two remaining parts, we widen a **gutter-like channel** <note>original: *hafran mīzābiyyan*. A U-shaped or rectangular groove.</note> along the entire length, the size of which should be half of the remaining thickness. 

Then we take an iron rod and wind it around the screw-grooves. We then mount it on the peg that contains the carving. We place its end into the screw-grooves after tightening the two pieces very well so that one stays fixed <gloss>3</gloss> <note>original: *bil-ham*. The manuscript variant suggests "by joining/soldering."</note> to the other, and there is no gap between them at all. Then we take...

<meta>This page continues the technical description of screw-based machinery, transitioning from the construction of the screw itself to its practical application in a pressing machine (likely for olives or grapes). It describes the interaction between the "female screw" beam and the press-box assembly.</meta>

—·( 113 )·—

...the surface of the **press-box** <term>press-box</term> <note>original: *al-ghalālaghra*. This is an Arabicization of the Greek word *galalagra*, a specific component of ancient presses used to contain the material being squeezed.</note> which fills the table; that is, it is inserted into it. The press-box is fitted into this hollow, and then we place a board on top of it, sized to fit it perfectly.

We then mount upon it a **block** <term>block</term> <note>original: *qurmiya*. Literally a stump or thick piece of wood used as a plinth to transmit pressure.</note> that is smaller in length and width than the board, with a thickness that fills the press-box. Next, we turn the two screws using the **lever-pins** <note>original: *al-awtād*. These are the wooden handles inserted into the head of the screw to provide leverage.</note> located in the **capstan-head** <term>capstan-head</term> <note>original: *al-falka*. Usually a disk or whorl; here it refers to the circular top of the screw with holes for the lever-pins.</note> until the beam containing the **female screw** <note>The internal threading within the wooden cross-beam.</note> descends upon the block. 

It then compresses the block and the board inside the press-box, **squeezing** <note>The manuscript contains a typo here: *f-y-'-r-ṣ* instead of *f-y-'-ṣ-r*.</note> the material within the press-box, causing the liquids to flow out. Then the screw is turned in the opposite direction, the beam rises, and the block is removed. The squeezed material is then replaced [with fresh material] until every bit of liquid has been extracted. ❦ {20} ❦

There may be another type of machine that uses only a single screw. This is done by preparing two upright pillars on the table which support a cross-beam containing the female screw-hole. Let the screw-hole be in the middle of this beam. The screw is then inserted into this hole and turned by the pins in the capstan-head until the screw descends upon the board fitted over the press-box, compressing it so that the liquids flow.

One should continue tightening it again and again until no liquid remains in the squeezed material. There are many other types of **presses** <note>original: *ma'āṣir*.</note> besides these, but we have chosen not to describe them because they are in common use among the general public and are familiar to them; furthermore, they are inferior to the ones we have mentioned <insert>in</insert> <note>The word "in" was omitted in the original manuscript.</note> their operation. ❦

...very much, but its pressure is also not continuous or strong; therefore, it must be attended to from time to time by turning and tightening. [14] These machines whose construction we are now describing are useful for **oil pressing** <term>oil pressing</term> <note>original: *ʿaṣr al-zayt*. The planks mounted above it collect the liquid <note>The translator suggests reading *tajma‘uhu* (collects it) for the manuscript’s unclear *tajanatuhu*.</note> very well, because when the grapes are pressed, the squeezed juice flows down. The **slats** <term>slats</term> <note>original: *al-masāṭir*. [17] As for the other **galalagra** <term>galalagra</term> <note>original: *al-ghālālāghrā*. This is a transliteration of the Greek *galalagra*, referring to the wooden cage or crate that holds the grapes during pressing.</note>, its four walls are made connected to one another by three cross-beams in each one. It is necessary for these four beams to be joined at their sides, fitted into a notch <note>Reading *mafrūḍan* (notched) for the manuscript’s *maqrūnan*.</note> reaching half of their thickness, so that when they are assembled together, the four walls stand perfectly fitted. In mechanical texts, this refers to a heavy wooden pressing-block or "plock" that sits on top of the fruit.</note> on its upper board, having a height as we mentioned earlier, so that the grapes do not become stuck <note>The translator reads *yu‘allaqa* (to hang/be suspended) for the manuscript’s *yuqāla*.</note> and the block sinks properly into the bottom of the *galalagra*.

[18] Now we shall examine the construction of the **presses** <term>presses</term> <note>original: *al-ma‘āṣir*.</note> which squeeze with great force and power. We will mention the distinction we previously made regarding the **lever** <term>lever</term> <note>original: *al-mikhīl*, derived from the Greek *mochlos*.</note>; these are among the strongest and most durable <note>The translator suggests *abqā* (durable) for the manuscript’s *wāsi‘ah* (wide).</note> types. First, we examine the difference between them, then we describe their form. We say that the wood called the *lever* is nothing but the site where a weight exerts pressure. The weight that presses is at an end elevated from the ground; when it presses, the fluids do not stop flowing until the weight rests on the ground. As for these machines whose description we are providing, they are powerful.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of pressing machinery, specifically detailing the construction of the galalagra (pressing cage) as an alternative to using ropes or baskets. This follows the general discussion of the components of the press seen on previous pages.</meta>

->( 108 )<-

[16] Some people have devised other methods for extracting juices. In place of the rope that is wound around packed grapes, or the **baskets** <note>original: *al-qifāf*. Woven containers, often made of palm fiber or esparto grass, used to hold crushed fruit.</note> in which olives are placed after being crushed and set under the rope-beam, they use a wooden device called a **galalagra** <term>galalagra</term> <note>original: *ghālālāghrā*. A transliteration of the Greek *galalagra*, referring to a wooden crate or cage that holds the fruit during the pressing process.</note>. They fill it with whatever they wish to press and place it under the beam known as the **lever-beam** <term>lever-beam</term> <note>original: *al-muḥabbil*. Literally the "rope-beam," this is the heavy horizontal timber that applies pressure to the fruit.</note>, then they lower the beam onto it. By doing this, they achieve the capacity they desire for pressing and find the work much easier.

There are two ways to construct this **galalagra**. The first type is "assembled." For this construction, one takes naturally hard and dense wood and makes **slats** <term>slats</term> <note>original: *masāṭir*. These are the horizontal planks or laths that form the walls of the pressing cage.</note> from it. Their length should be according to the size of the machine we wish to build, their width about two spans, and their thickness about six fingers. 

Then, we cut a notch into both ends of every slat on both sides. After leaving a margin of six fingers from the edge, we cut the notch at the top, penetrating to a depth of a quarter of the slat’s thickness. We do the same at the bottom, so that the wood remaining in the middle is half of its original thickness. The notches in the slats must be equal so that they can be fitted into one another. We then assemble the slats until their combination forms an equilateral square shape, similar to a **chest** <note>original: *al-tābūt*. A box or rectangular container.</note>. The gaps between the joined slats should be wide so that the liquids may flow out of them quickly. In this machine, there is no need for the wood that is usually placed on the grapes <note>This likely refers to the loose planks used in rope-pressing, as the cage and its specific pressing-block replace them.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing a screw-press mechanism. While the previous page focused on the "galalagra" or pressing cage, this page describes the assembly of the screw (*al-lawlab*) into its wooden housing and its connection to a heavy stone base, which acts as the weight or anchor for the pressing action.</meta>

->( 107 )<-

...solid and uncarved. When we fit the end of the **screw** <term>screw</term> <note>original: *al-lawlab*. A large wooden or metal screw used to generate mechanical pressure.</note> into the end of the strong timber whose internal threading has been carved to its maximum depth—or slightly less—the screw passes entirely into the hollowed timber until it is completely hidden. 

When we have done this, we carve a circular groove into the neck of this internally-hollowed timber, just a short distance from its tip, and we fit **iron rings** <note>original: *khawātim ḥadīd*. These act as bushings or reinforcements to reduce friction and prevent the wood from splitting.</note> onto it, just as is done with the axles of wheels. Then we carve a hole into the **stone** <note>The heavy base or counterweight of the press.</note> large enough for the end of this timber to be fitted into it. This should be done so that it is possible for the timber to rotate easily. 

We then insert the end of the timber into that hole and provide it with **iron clamps** <note>original: *ḍibāb ḥadīd*. Metal brackets or plates used to secure the components.</note> to prevent the timber from pulling out of the hole in the stone. We also place an iron ring on the designated circular groove at the end of the timber to ensure its rotation is smooth. Above this assembly in the stone, we place crosswise holes from which four ends of **pegs** <term>pegs</term> <note>original: *awtād*. These serve as handles for the operators to grip and turn the mechanism.</note> emerge. 

Once we have done that, if we wish to use the timber called the **lever** <term>lever</term> <note>original: *lakhīl*. A transliteration of the Greek *mokhloi*, referring to the bars or levers used to turn the screw.</note>, we connect the ends of the screw to the internally-hollowed timber. Then the four pegs are turned until the screw passes into the hollow, which presses the timber down and lifts the stone, thereby squeezing everything beneath the timber <gloss>The manuscript says "stone" (*al-ḥajar*) here, but it is likely a scribal error for "timber" (*al-khashaba*)</gloss>. 

When the timber has descended until it rests on the ground, we turn it in the opposite direction until the timber rises and the stone is fixed in place. This method of operation is strong, reliable, and safe in its result, and it does not require much labor.

<summary>This page describes the final assembly of a screw-press, detailing how the screw is housed in a threaded timber and anchored to a stone base using iron rings and clamps for smooth rotation. The mechanism allows for high-pressure extraction with minimal physical effort by using levers to turn the screw.</summary>

<keywords>screw-press, mechanical engineering, levers, iron bushings, stone base, pressure extraction, ancient machinery</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical specifications for assembling a mechanical press, specifically focusing on the interface between the screw and its housing. —•••( 106 )•••—

the **screw** <term>screw</term> <note>original: *al-lawlab*. A threaded mechanical component.</note> from one side. As for the other side, the screw-thread carving must reach the very end of the screw-timber. The remaining portion of the timber is to be made square. Within this square section, we hollow out a cavity called a **tarmas** <note>original: *tarmas*, likely a transliteration of the Greek *tormos* (τόρμος), meaning a socket, hole, or mortise.</note>, which is a circular hole carved into the end of the wood so that the wood can be joined with the timber to which it must be connected. 

Then, we fit this **socket** <note>original: *tarmas*.</note> into one side of the **plinth** <term>plinth</term> <note>original: *al-līna*. In mechanical texts of this era, this refers to the heavy base-block or sole-plate of the press.</note> which sits beneath the timber. We then use transverse iron nails, fitting their ends into this carved cavity, and we **nail** <gloss>original manuscript reading: "we nail it" (nasmaru-hā)</gloss> it to the plinth. We also use an iron axle that passes through this socket and exits into the plinth; we tighten it there to increase its stability and the strength of its connection to the wooden plinth. 

Next, we use another square piece of wood made from a hard, strong timber. Its length should be equal to the length of the **screw**, and its width—measured by the side of its square base—must be larger than the diameter of the screw cylinder by an amount sufficient to allow that cylinder to be fitted inside this square timber. 

We then split this timber lengthwise into two halves. In each of the two parts, we carve a rounded, gutter-like groove to create the **female screw** <term>female screw</term> <note>original: *unthā al-lawlab*. This is the nut or the threaded housing that receives the screw.</note>. We carve it with screw-threads so that the **male screw** <note>original: *al-lawlab al-dhakar*. The threaded rod itself.</note> can be fitted into it. Then, we join the two halves together so that they become a single unit. 

The screw-thread carving in the female timber should also be **hollowed through** <gloss>original manuscript reading: "hollowed through" (nāfidhan)</gloss> on one side until the end of the timber; however, on the other side, it leaves...

<meta>This page continues the technical description of a grape press, specifically addressing the mechanical disadvantages of using ropes and pulleys to lift the heavy counterweight stone. It transitions into a more reliable design using a screw mechanism. This text precedes the description of the screw-socket (tormos) assembly found on page 106.</meta>

...and the stone called **lis** <note>original: *lis*. A technical name for the heavy counterweight stone in a press.</note> is raised by it. However, the stiffness of the rope creates a certain resistance to the lowering of the beam and the rising of the stone. This is because when the rope is stiff, it does not glide easily over the **pulley** <term>pulley</term> <note>original: *al-bakara*.</note> when raising the beam to its highest point. 

Furthermore, for the lowering of the beam and the lifting of the stone, we are required to use long **pegs** <note>original: *awtād*. These are the handles or spokes inserted into the axle to turn it.</note> to rotate the **axle** <term>axle</term> <note>original: *al-miḥwar*.</note>. We cannot be certain of safety—especially when the heap of **pressed grapes** <note>original: *al-‘inab al-marṣūṣ*.</note> beneath the beam is large and many men are gathered to turn the axle—that the pegs will not break and fall, causing them injury; or they might slip out of the holes and fall, with the same result.

Therefore, they devised another method that does not require a rope, which is easier <gloss>The manuscript reads "leisurely" (*mahal*), but "easier" (*ashal*) is the intended meaning.</gloss> and more reliable than the previous one. This is its construction: 

We use a piece of square wood shaped like a **block** <term>block</term> <note>original: *al-labina*, literally "the brick." In this context, a wooden plinth or mounting block.</note>. We fit it under the beam called the **mukhiyyal** <term>lever-beam</term> <note>original: *al-mukhiyyal*, a transliteration of the Greek *mochlós*. The primary horizontal beam of the press used to apply pressure.</note> at the position where the rope used to be. Let the part of this block that faces upward be rounded. On each of the two sides of the fixed corner, place **guides** <note>original: *lāghānāt*. These are likely channels or slots intended to keep the block aligned during movement.</note> fixed upon the beam called the *mukhiyyal*. This is to ensure that the block does not move more than is required, while still allowing it to tilt toward both sides. 

Then, we raise the beam to its highest possible point for the setting of the *mukhiyyal*, and we measure the distance between the block and the stone. We take half of this measurement, or slightly more, and create a **lenticular screw** <note>original: *lawlab ‘adasī*. Literally a "lens-like" screw; this refers to the shape of the thread or the screw-head in Greek mechanical traditions.</note> of moderate thickness to this length. Let the carving of the screw-thread not reach the very end of the timber...

<summary>This page explains the safety hazards and mechanical inefficiencies of using ropes to lift press weights, proposing instead a screw-based system. It describes the installation of a wooden mounting block (labina) and the initial measurements for the main screw.</summary>
<keywords>grape press, mechanical advantage, pulley, axle, lever-beam, screw-thread, Heron of Alexandria, safety in engineering</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of a grape press. While the previous page discussed the mechanical disadvantages of ropes and pulleys, this section defines the essential components of the press—the lever-beam, the vat, and the counterweight—and describes a specific pulley-and-axle system used to lift the massive stone weights required for high-pressure extraction.</meta>

Regarding the **use of weights**, it is necessary that we demonstrate and explain enough of the subject to satisfy what is required to know. As for the timber called the **lever-beam** <term>lever-beam</term> <note>original: *khayl*, likely a transliteration of the Greek *mokhloi*. The manuscript scribe often mistakenly wrote *ḥabl* (rope) instead.</note>—which other people call the **press-beam** <term>press-beam</term> <note>original: *‘aṣṣār*.</note>—it is nothing other than the pivot point and the stone foundation beneath it; this is the wall of the press-house where the end of the beam is secured. The "pressure" is the force applied to the mass of crushed grapes, and the "moving force" is the stone suspended from the end of the timber called the **press-vat** <term>press-vat</term> <note>original: *līn*, from the Greek *lēnós*.</note>. It is often required that large timbers be used so that the weight of the wood itself is great enough to assist in the pressing. For a truly large beam, the length might reach twenty-five cubits <note>Approximately 11–12 meters.</note>, and the suspended stone—which is called the **lithos** <term>stone-weight</term> <note>original: *libās*, a transcription of the Greek *lāas*, meaning "stone."</note>—may weigh as much as twenty **quintals** <term>quintal</term> <note>original: *qinṭār*. A large unit of weight; twenty quintals would be roughly 900–1,000 kilograms.</note>.

[١٤] We wish to devise a method for managing the stone, so we employ this operation: we take a large lifting machine and fix a **pulley** <term>pulley</term> <note>original: *bakara*.</note> to the end of the lever-beam and another pulley to the stone. Above the pulley on the stone, we fasten a cross-beam, suspending it from the timber called the lever-beam. We then lead the rope to an **axle** <term>axle</term> <note>original: *miḥwar*.</note> upon which there is a **winch-wheel** <term>wheel</term> <note>original: *falka*.</note>. We turn the wheel, which causes the rope to wrap around the axle, and the stone is raised.

[١٥] Another device may be found to lower the beam called...

<meta>This page transitions from the specific mechanics of the grape press discussed on the previous page to broader engineering solutions for transporting massive objects and repairing structural damage caused by natural disasters. It concludes the section on heavy lifting before moving into agricultural machinery.</meta>

They took two ships <note>original: *asfīn*, a variation of *safīnatayn* (two ships) found in the manuscript.</note> and secured them with heavy cables <term>cable</term> <note>original: *qalūs*, a thick nautical rope used for towing.</note> on both sides of the ring-collar <term>ring-collar</term> <note>original: *ṭawq*. This likely refers to a large structural stone or a specialized transport frame for a heavy object like an obelisk.</note> within its walls. Then they prepared the load upon the ring-collar, loosened the jars <term>jars</term> <note>original: *balālīs*, large ceramic containers often used as floats or to manage ballast.</note>, and let the sand flow out. They then steered the two ships into the sea, and the weight of the ring-collar was successfully carried. ۩ 

[١٢] There were also people who devised a way to submerge <note>original: *yanḥadirū*. The manuscript reading was unclear, but likely refers to the controlled lowering of stones.</note> massive stones into the sea using this same method. Others devised a way to raise walls that had tilted during earthquakes using a similar approach. They would dig into the ground on the side toward which the wall had leaned, making a trench along the length of the wall. Then they placed square timber into it, at a short distance from the wall. They erected another piece of timber, standing upright, between the wall and the square timber they had placed in the trench. Then they attached pulleys <term>pulley</term> <note>original: *bakrah*.</note> to the ends of the upright timber and passed ropes through them to a machine called a **winch** <term>winch</term> <note>original: *malqah*, a mechanical device for pulling or hoisting.</note>. They turned that machine until the ropes were pulled taut, drawing the cross-beam with them. As the cross-beam was pulled, the upright timber was also drawn, which forced the wall back into its original position. Once they had returned it to its place, they left it secured by that timber for a period of time so that the stones could settle against one another. Then they removed the timber, and the wall remained stable in its upright <note>original: *al-manṣūbah*.</note> position. ۩ 

[١٣] As for what is required concerning the movement of heavy weights and the methods that are useful for that purpose, we have provided a sufficient explanation. Now, regarding agricultural tools—specifically those used to press juices <term>juices</term> <note>original: *al-andiyah*, literally "moistures" or "liquids."</note> and oils—they are not far removed from the principles we have already mentioned.

### Page 102

[10] Some people have also attempted, using this same method, to raise great pillars <term>pillars</term> <note>original: *asāṭīn*, derived from the Greek *stylos*, referring to large architectural columns.</note> so that they sit upon their bases in the desired location. By this device, they tied ropes <note>the manuscript clarifies the word is *ḥibālan* (ropes).</note> to the head of the pillar they wished to raise and passed them through pulleys <term>pulley</term> <note>original: *bakrah*.</note> secured to a fixed tower. 

After the ropes were passed through to the other side of the pulleys, they attached containers—by which I mean chests or similar vessels—to the ends. They then placed enough stones and heavy weights into those containers to equal or exceed the weight of the timber <note>original: *‘ūd*. Though the text discusses stone pillars, "timber" may refer to the wooden crane or the transport frame encasing the stone.</note>. When this balance is achieved, the weight lifts the pillar so that it stands upright upon its base. It is necessary to tie the bottom of the pillar to its base so it does not slip or shift when lifted. Alternatively, one might wrap thick cables <term>cable</term> <note>original: *qalūs*, a heavy nautical rope.</note> around the base of the pillar like a crown, so that when the pillar is raised, its bottom remains steady within those encircling cables.

[11] Others have attempted to lower <note>original: *yanḥadirū*. The manuscript contains a clerical error here, possibly *yaḥiddū*.</note> massive loads into the sea using this trick. They constructed a high raft <term>raft</term> <note>original: *ṭawf*.</note> from wood, fastened together with nails and pegs, and gave it sturdy walls for strength in the area where they intended to carry the weight. Beneath the raft, they placed sacks <term>sacks</term> <note>original: *balālīs*, a term of uncertain origin likely referring to large jars, skins, or heavy bags used for buoyancy.</note> filled with sand, with their openings <note>the manuscript provides a conjecture for *al-afwāh* (openings/mouths) over *al-akwām* (heaps).</note> tightly closed. They then set the raft upon these sacks, and then—

<meta>This page transitions from the general theory of lifting mechanisms to a specific case study in civil engineering: the controlled descent of massive stones from mountain quarries. —( 101 )—

### Page 101

[9] As for the methods by which we raise or elevate <note>original: *narfa‘u bihā aw nu‘allī*</note> heavy objects, they are those we have already mentioned. It is also necessary to devise strategies regarding the location, the timing, and other requirements, explaining how each of these should be employed.

For instance, some people have used a clever device for lowering large stones <note>original: *iḥdār al-ḥijāra al-kibār*</note> from the peaks of towering mountains. This was done so that the steepness of the mountain slope <note>the manuscript uses *al-inṣibāb*, referring to the incline or "pouring" downward of the path.</note> would not cause the stone to rush down by its own momentum and strike the animals pulling it or the wagon, thereby destroying them. 

To achieve this, they prepared two paths on the mountain in the place where they intended to move the stone from the top to the bottom, smoothing the paths as much as possible. They took two four-wheeled wagons <term>wagon</term> <note>original: *‘ajala*, a wheeled vehicle or carriage.</note> and placed one of them at the top of the path intended for the stone's descent, and the other at the bottom of the second path. Then, they secured a pulley <term>pulley</term> <note>original: *bakrah*, a simple machine used to change the direction of a force.</note> to a fixed pillar <note>original: *rukn thābit*.</note> situated between the two paths. They passed ropes <note>the translator adds the word "from" (*min*) here for clarity.</note> from the wagon carrying the stone to the pulley, and then through to the other wagon at the bottom. 

Into that lower wagon, they placed small stones—specifically the stone-chips <note>original: *najārat al-ḥajar*, literally the "shavings" or debris from carving the large stone.</note> produced during the carving of the great block—until the wagon was weighted just slightly less than the heavy stone they intended to lower. They then hitched animals to that lower wagon to pull it upward. As a result of that wagon ascending bit by bit, the great stone descended toward the bottom in a slow, easy manner, moving only a little at a time.

<summary>This page describes an ancient engineering solution for safely transporting massive stones down steep mountain slopes using a dual-track system, a pulley, and a counterweighted wagon to prevent runaway accidents.</summary>
<keywords>mechanical engineering, stone transport, counterweight, pulley, wagons, ancient technology, Qusta ibn Luqa, Hero of Alexandria</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of a heavy-lifting apparatus, specifically focusing on the assembly and material requirements of the three-part "lewis bolt" mechanism used to hoist large masonry blocks into place.</meta>

—( 100 )—

... the other two wedges. We install an axle-hole in the third wedge, such that one of its ends is thick. Thus, the three wedges will have filled the *ferrachit* <term>ferrachit</term> <note>original: *farrākhīṭ*, from the Greek *parachitēs*. This refers to a dovetail-shaped mortise cut into the top of a stone block for lifting.</note> cavity. The curved ends of the two [outer] wedges will have entered the hollow on the sides of the cavity’s surface, while the third wedge fills the space between the two wedges. Consequently, the three wedges become like a single solid body. 

Next, a rope <term>rope</term> <note>original: *qalūs*, a heavy-duty cable or hawser.</note> is fastened to that axle that passes through the three wedges, in which there is a pulley <term>pulley</term> <note>original: *bakrah*.</note>. At the top of the machine used for lifting the weight, there is another pulley aligned with the one on the stone. We pass the ropes through it and pull; the stone then rises because the middle wedge does not allow the two wedges with curved ends inside the stone to give way. 

It is then raised until it aligns with the place where we wish to install it, and it is set down upon that spot. Once the stone is seated in its place, the axle <note>original: *miḥwar*.</note> is removed, the middle wedge is pulled out, and each of the curved-ended wedges is taken out. Then we prepare another stone and perform the same operation as the first.

One must take care in this work to avoid using iron that is too hard <note>original: *ṣulb*, implying brittleness.</note>, lest it snap. One must also avoid soft iron, lest it bend and give way under the weight of the stone. Instead, one should use iron of a medium quality—neither extremely hard nor extremely soft. 

Care should also be taken against any warping of the iron, or any folding or cracking that might occur during its manufacture. A mistake in this is very grave, not only because the stone might fall, but because it also harms the craftsmen <note>original: *al-ṣunnā‘*, the skilled laborers or builders.</note> if it falls.
