...as the shape of a ladder <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: *al-sullam*. The translator notes this word might actually mean a "hook" or "clasp" in this technical context.</note>. We have set up this ladder on one side to support the load, and cross-beams—meaning at the ends of the upright posts—were placed at its ends and secured with ropes. When it is raised, it carries the load. It is necessary to place cross-beams at the ends of these upright posts to join them together outside of the stone at their extremities, so that when the stone is suspended from them, it does not tilt and cause the stone to fall. These cross-beams should bind the posts to one another and be secured by the ropes that extend from them to the pulley <term>pulley</term> <note>original: *bakrah*</note>; thus, when the ropes are pulled, they lift the stone.

[8] We also employ another method for this task which is easier and more reliable than the first. This refers to a "dovetail" cut, where the bottom of the hole is wider than the top opening.</note>—meaning that it should have a sufficient hollow at the bottom on both sides. This hollowed area must be solid enough to pull the stone attached to it.

We then bring a third iron wedge and install it between these two wedges to prevent them from shaking or shifting. The drawn shape is such that each of the two surfaces **Q Z H T** is a parallelogram, and let **Q Z H T** be greater in width than **K L M B**. As for the length, let them be equal, meaning that line **K M** is equal to line **Q H** <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: *qāf-ḥā’*. These letters refer to a geometric diagram not present on this specific page.</note>. We then carve this shape into the depth of the stone, and let the depth of the carving be according to the weight of the stone. Let the carving of surface **Q Z H T** be right-angled, carved with precision. 

As for the surface **K L M B**, it should be slanted <term>slanted</term> <note>original: *mūrib*. In this context, it refers to an undercut or dovetail cut where the interior is wider than the opening.</note>—meaning its bottom is wider than its top. The carving should be in the fashion of a "wooden lock" <note>This is a historical term for a dovetail joint.</note>, where the narrow part of it is equal to **K L M B** and the wide part is equal to **Q Z H T**. 

We then fashion a body for them also in the shape of a "wooden lock" made of iron, which fits into this carving. At its top, there should be a ring attached to it. It is inserted into the **Q Z H T** carving until it passes through, then it is pushed and turned until it settles in the "female" <note>The hollowed-out part of the joint.</note> carving so that it cannot be pulled out. Then, wooden wedges are fitted over the **Q Z H T** carving so that <gloss>2</gloss> the iron piece cannot be pushed out. 

We then attach the ropes—which used to carry the sling that the stone would sit in—to the ring connected to the iron pin <term>iron pin</term> <note>original: *al-watad*. Here it refers to the central bolt of the lifting assembly.</note>. By this process, the stone is lifted until it reaches the desired position without anything obstructing it. Once the stone is settled in its place, those wedges are removed, the iron piece is withdrawn, and it is then fitted into another stone. ¤

### [5] The Four-Legged Machine
As for the machine that possesses four legs, it is used for weights that are excessive in size. Its construction requires four pillars made of wood <margin>pillars of wood</margin> to be erected so that their layout forms a rectangle <term>parallelogram</term> <note>original: *mutawāzī al-aḍlā‘*. In classical mechanics, this refers to a rectangular base frame providing stability.</note>. Let its width be of a measure that allows the stone to move freely within it and be raised with ease. 

Then, we fasten beams across the tops of these pillars to connect them to one another; this must be done with precision and sturdiness. We then place another beam upon these beams, fastened in a way that crosses the arrangement of all the pillars. We then secure the pulley <term>pulley</term> <note>original: *bakrah*</note> in the center of this woodwork, at the exact mark where the beams meet one another. We then secure the stone with the ropes that are in the pulley and pull them so the load is raised. 

In all mechanical devices, one should take care to avoid using nails or pegs <note>original: *musāmīr wa-awtād*</note>, especially for massive weights, as they require boring holes [which weaken the wood]. Instead, we should use ropes <gloss>1</gloss> <note>The manuscript suggests "we use in the machines" as an alternative reading.</note> and thick cables <term>cables</term> <note>original: *quls*. A heavy maritime or construction rope.</note>. With these, we bind whatever we need in place of the things we would otherwise have nailed <gloss>2</gloss> <note>The manuscript here is somewhat unclear; the scribe may have intended "nailed" or "fixed."</note>.

A device consisting of iron pieces inserted into a specially carved hole in the stone.</note>. 

We mark a shape similar to a square upon the base of the stone, which is the surface **A B G D**...

We use the machine called an *Oudos* <note>original: οὐδός. A Greek term for a "threshold" or "sill," here referring to a horizontal base frame or sleeper beam that provides a foundation for the crane.</note> and we mount the pillars upon it. Let them incline toward the upper direction with a slight tilt, measuring approximately one-fifth of the distance from the bottom. We then fasten the two pillars onto this base-frame so that their ends are joined together. At the ends of the pillars, we place another crossbeam upon which other pulleys of great lifting power are fastened. Let there be other pulleys fastened to the stone [load], and the rope is extended for them as in the first operation, either by hand or by using mechanical devices <note>The manuscript originally read "with those having," but it has been corrected to "with machines."</note>, and the weight is raised. To ensure these pillars remain elevated, they must be tied with rope in the manner of the binding we described previously. Then, one places the stone and moves that base-frame to another part of the building according to the requirements of the work.

### [10] The Three-Legged Machine
As for the device <term>trick</term> <note>original: *hīlah*. In medieval mechanics, this refers to an "ingenious mechanical device."</note> that possesses three legs, it is constructed in this manner: we take three pillars and incline them toward one another so that their tops meet at a single point. At that mark where the three pillars meet <note>The manuscript originally used a masculine pronoun here, later corrected to feminine to agree with "mark."</note>, we fasten a pulley of great lifting power. Another part of the pulley is fastened to the load. When the ropes <term>cables</term> <note>original: *fulūs*. Likely referring to the strands or ropes passing through the pulleys.</note> of the pulley are pulled <note>The manuscript originally read "occurred," but has been corrected to "pulled."</note>, the load is raised. 

The base of this machine is more stable and safer than any other; however, it is not suitable for use in every location we might desire. It is only appropriate for places where we wish to raise the load directly into the center of the machine. If we need to move a load to a position where it is possible...

Therefore, we must consider the magnitude of the weight we wish to lift, so that the force of the weight does not exceed the strength of that upright pillar. We set that upright level upon a wooden beam in which it can pivot <note>The text implies the pillar is set into a socket or "disturbed" (loose) enough to allow for tilting.</note>, and we tie three or four ropes to the top of that pillar and secure them to fixed anchors that are extremely stable. We tighten the ropes upon them, and then we place a pulley at the end of this pillar, which is tied <gloss>to it</gloss> <note>original: *ilayhā*. The gloss corrects the manuscript's masculine pronoun to a feminine one to agree with "pulley".</note> with a rope. We then attach the cables <note>original: *qulūs*. Likely derived from the Greek *kalos*, referring to thick hauling-ropes.</note> that are in the pulley to the load we wish to move. Next, we pull the cables either by hand or by another machine.

When the load is raised, if you need <gloss>and you needed</gloss> to place the stone upon a wall or any position you desire, you loosen the rope attached to one of the fixed anchors which pulls the pillar—upon which the pulley is tied—toward the direction opposite to where you want to place the stone. Thus, the pillar will tilt toward that direction. Then you lower the rope that is in the pulley little by little until it reaches the place where you want to set it down.

If the tilt of the pillar carrying the pulley does not reach far enough to bring the lifted weight to the desired location, we place rounded wooden rollers beneath it to move it along, or we push it with the rope until it reaches the desired spot. Once we have done that, we return the pillar to its original position from the other side facing us, then we secure it again and use it as in the first operation. ۞

The sledges <term>khanat</term> <note>These are wooden cradles or frames used to support a heavy load while it is being moved.</note> and these cables <term>qulus</term> <note>Derived from the Greek *kalos*, referring to thick hauling ropes.</note> are moved either by hand or by other mechanical means. When the cables are pulled, the sledges travel along the ground. Sometimes, thin round wood <note>rollers</note> or planks are placed under the sledges for them to move upon. If the load is small, round wood should be used. However, if the weight is massive, then planks should be used instead, because they do not move as quickly. This is because round wood, when rolling under a very heavy load, may shatter <note>literally: "be pounded"</note> under the weight due to the speed and intensity of its movement. Some people use neither planks nor round wood, but instead install solid wheels <note>original: *falakan*, referring to disks or pulleys used as wheels.</note> at the edges of the sledges to move upon. ۝

[2] To lift heavy objects to a height, certain mechanical devices <term>hiyal</term> <note>The Arabic term *hiyal* (ingenious devices) was the classical name for the science of mechanics.</note> are required. Some of these have a single upright <term>qā'ima</term>, some have two uprights, some have three, and some have four. As for the machine with a single upright, it is constructed in this manner: We take a long beam of wood that is taller than the height to which we wish to lift the weight. If this beam is strong in itself, we take a cable, tie it to the beam, and wrap it around at equal intervals. Let the distance between each wrap of the rope be about four spans <gloss>four spans</gloss> <note>The manuscript originally read "four things," but has been corrected to "spans" (the distance from the thumb to the little finger).</note>. This increases the strength of the beam, and the wrapping of the cable serves as steps for anyone who needs to climb to the top of the beam to fasten something, which makes the work much easier. However, if that beam is not inherently strong...

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book III (The Third Discourse) of Heron’s Mechanics. While the previous page provided for continuity (numbered 92 in its own sequence) discussed the specific mechanics of cranes, this text serves as the formal introduction to the third book. It bridges the theoretical study of the "five powers" (simple machines) from the previous books with the practical application of moving and lifting heavy weights on the ground.</meta>

## The Third Discourse of the Book of Heron
### On the Lifting of Heavy Objects

[١] In the discourse preceding this one, we discussed the <term>five powers</term> <note>These are the five simple machines described by Heron: the lever, the windlass (wheel and axle), the pulley, the wedge, and the screw.</note> and explained the principles by which great weights are moved with little force. We established more on that subject than those who came before us, and we explained the reason why extreme power in machines is accompanied by <term>slowness</term> <note>This refers to the fundamental law of mechanics: what is gained in force is lost in speed or distance.</note>. We also explained other things that will benefit students of <term>ingenious devices</term> <note>original: *al-hiyal*. This is the classical Arabic term for the science of mechanics, implying "tricks" or "clever arrangements" used to overcome natural limits.</note>, providing sufficient instruction for those learning.

In this discourse, we shall write of methods that are useful in facilitating the practical <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: *wujud*, literally "existence" or "presence."</note> application of what we previously discussed, which also assist in the movement of heavy bodies. Furthermore, we will describe machines used for <term>pressing</term> <note>Referring to wine or olive presses.</note>, as these also require great force to operate.

As for objects that are dragged along the ground, they are pulled upon <term>sledges</term> <gloss>2</gloss> <note>original: *al-khanat*. This is likely a transliteration of the Greek word *chelōnē*, meaning "tortoise," which refers to a protective wooden frame or cradle used to move heavy loads.</note> and <term>tortoise-frames</term> <gloss>3</gloss> <note>original: *al-lajaat*, meaning a species of tortoise. In a mechanical context, this is a "testudo" or a heavy wooden base.</note>. These are stable bodies made of squared timber with notched ends. The weights are placed upon these sledges, and ropes or other designated attachments are fastened to their ends for pulling.

---

1 <note>original manuscript reads: "its existence" (wujuduhu).</note>
2 <note>original: *al-khanat*. This is a doubtful word in the manuscript. The manuscript uses *al-lahamat* twice and *al-lajanat* four times. It is transcribed here as *al-khanat* on the assumption that it is a faulty transcription of the Greek *chelōnē* (tortoise-frame).</note>
3 <note>The second word, *al-lajaat*, appears correct; it signifies a species of tortoise (Latin: *testudo*).</note>

<meta>This page continues Heron of Alexandria’s exploration of the "center of gravity," a concept he helped formalize. Weight **J** is to weight **B** as line **BD** is to line **DJ**. The weight located at **D** is <term>given</term> <note>original: *malfuz*. In this context, it refers to a known or "expressed" value, corresponding to the Greek mathematical term *rhetos*.</note>. Therefore, the two weights at **B** and **J** are given. Since the weight at **A** is also given, it follows that the weights at the <term>vertices</term> <note>original: *al-qawa’im*. Literally "the uprights" or "the pillars," here referring to the corners or vertices of the geometric frame.</note> are all given.

**[40]** We wish to determine how, if there is a triangle **ABJ** and there are known weights at the points **A**, **B**, and **J**, we may find a point inside the triangle from which it may be suspended so that the triangle remains in <term>equilibrium</term> <note>original: *ya’tadil*. Meaning to be balanced or level.</note>. We divide line **AB** at point **D** in a ratio such that line **BD** is to **DA** as the weight at **A** is to the weight at **B**. Thus, the center of gravity for the combination of these two weights lies at point **D**. Let us then join the two points **D** and **J** with the line **DJ**, and divide it at point **E** in a ratio such that line **JE** is to **ED** as the weight at **D** is to the weight at **J**. Point **E** will then be the <term>center of gravity</term> <note>original: *markaz al-thiqal*.</note> for the entire combined group. Therefore, this is the point of suspension.

**[41]** We wish to demonstrate this same principle for a <term>polygonal</term> <note>original: *kathir al-adla’*. Literally "many-sided."</note> shape. Let the shape **ABJDE** be a polygon. Let us suspend known weights at the points **A**, **B**, **J**, **D**, and **E**. We divide line **AB** at point **Z** in a ratio such that line **BZ** is to **ZA** as weight **A** is to weight **B**. Thus, point **Z** is the center of the two weights at **A** and **B**. We also divide line **DE** at point **H** in a ratio such that line <unclear>DH</unclear> <note>The OCR here contains a repetition "DH HE" which likely indicates a ratio segment.</note> is to line **HE** as weight **E** is to weight **D**. We then join **Z** and **H** with a line and divide it at point **T** in a ratio such that the sum of **AB** is to the sum of **DE** as line **HT** is to **TZ**. Let us connect points **A-E** and **G-J** and divide them at points **Z** and **H** in a way that **AZ** is twice **ZE**, and **JH** is twice **HE**. <note>This follows the geometric principle that the center of gravity of a triangle is located on the median, two-thirds of the way from the vertex to the base.</note> Thus, the <term>center of gravity</term> <note>original: *markaz al-thiqal*</note> of triangle **ABE** falls on point **Z**, and the center of triangle **BGDJ** falls on point **H**. ### [37]
We wish to demonstrate this same principle for a pentagon **ABGDE**. Let us connect **BE** and find the center of gravity of triangle **ABE**, and let it fall on point **Z**. Let us connect points **ZH** and divide line **ZH** into two parts such that the ratio of segment **HT** to **TZ** is as the ratio of the weight of triangle **ABE** to the weight of quadrilateral **BGDE**. Point **T** will then be the center of gravity for the shape **ABGDE**. In this manner, one should conceive of the center for every many-sided shape <note>i.e., any polygon</note>.

Regarding spread sails <gloss>1 ms. variant: "vultures" or "eagles"</gloss>: Because when the intensity of motion reaches an object that yields to it and does not resist it, it does not produce a strong effect. This is because the speed of the movement and the magnitude of the force are dispersed upon meeting a yielding, non-resisting object. However, when a solid object meets another solid object like itself and strikes it, the solid object does not yield but resists it. Thus, none of its force is dispersed, and the strike upon it is very powerful. For this reason, those who throw themselves from a great height into the water are not harmed by its impact <note>The author is comparing the "yielding" nature of water or air to the "resistance" of solid ground.</note>. ۝

**17** Why is it that fluids, though they are heavy by nature, move quickly and easily? For we see a single man move a thousand <term>qist: an ancient unit of liquid measure, roughly equivalent to 1.1 liters</term> of water at once. This is because water is continuous and its parts are quick to separate. It is not like stone or wood, which are dense <gloss>2 ms. compacted</gloss> and difficult to divide; rather, it is easy to separate. Therefore, it has no stability in itself but is flowing <gloss>3 ms. liquid/running</gloss> downwards. It follows from this that when we move a tiny part of it, all its other parts incline toward the place where that tiny part was shifted. ۝

### 14
Why is it that things traveling in the water move with greater speed if they are on a single "wall" <note>original: *ha'it wahid*; likely referring to a single hull or a specific alignment of the vessel's side against the water</note>? This is because the part of it that is in the water is very small, so the support it receives from the water is also small. Thus, the wind that catches it overcomes the water that resists its movement. ۝

### 15

### 10
**Why is it that scales, when they are rotated in a circle, whether they are heavy or light, move faster than their motion toward one of the sides to which they incline?**

Because if the scale is entirely rotated <gloss>1. *kulluha*, as it appears in the manuscript.</gloss>, its weight is equal <gloss>2. The manuscript carries the form *mutasawiha* (its equality), but it should be read as "equal".</gloss> from all sides. It therefore moves upon a center, and its center <gloss>3. *markazuhu*, as it appears in the manuscript.</gloss> is its point of suspension. But if we pull the scale toward one of the two sides, we are lifting a certain weight, because the downward inclination of one pan raises the other. This results in an unnatural movement—I mean the movement of a weight toward the heights. As for natural movement <term>natural movement (*al-harakah al-tabi'iyyah*): in Aristotelian physics, the "natural" tendency of heavy objects to move toward the center of the earth</term>, it is easy, and it consists of pulling the weight downward. For this reason, pulling weights downward is easier than drawing <gloss>4. *raddana* (pulling back/returning), as it appears in the manuscript.</gloss> them upward.

### 11
**Why has the movement of suspended weights become easy?**

Because the total force of the weight has been overcome by the force from which it is suspended. Since not much force remains within the weight itself, pushing it becomes easy. This likewise occurs in the scale; when it is suspended and we pull it, it moves more easily.

### 12
**Why are the large stones that roll down to the seashore mostly round?**

Because they were originally full of sharp angles...

<summary>This page explores early mechanical physics, specifically why circular rotation in a balanced scale is faster than vertical movement and why suspended objects are easier to move. It concludes with the beginning of an inquiry into why coastal erosion rounds the edges of large stones.</summary>
<keywords>mechanics, scales, circular motion, natural movement, suspended weights, geometry of stones, Aristotelian physics</keywords>

This page concludes a technical discussion on the tension and length of wood before transitioning to Problem 9 (in this manuscript's numbering), which applies the principle of the lever to the medical practice of tooth extraction.</meta>

->( 83 )<-

...much force scattered throughout its parts, so they all prevail over the stationary part of it by which it is held. Thus, what happens to short pieces of wood happens to it when something is hung at their ends to press them down: the increase in the length of the wood is proportional to that weight which pulls the short wood. Thus, the long wood attains through its own length the same effect that the short wood attains when a heavy object is tied to its end.

### 9
**Why is the extraction of molars performed with forceps rather than by hand?**

Is it because we cannot grasp the tooth with the whole hand, but only with a portion of it? Just as it is more difficult for us to lift a weight with only two fingers than it is with the whole hand, it is likewise more difficult for us to grasp and compress an object with two fingers than with the whole hand. This is because the force is unified in all the helpers <note>The "helpers" refers to the fingers working together as a single unit of force.</note>.

Furthermore, the division of the forceps at its pivot <gloss>1. Lexicons usually provide *mismar* (nail or bolt) instead of the manuscript's *samar*</gloss> also allows the hand to prevail over the tooth, because the hand’s placement on the tool is at the greatest part <note>The "greatest part" refers to the long handles of the forceps, providing mechanical advantage.</note>. The length of the forceps helps in the movement of the tooth. This is because the root of the tooth is the point upon which the fulcrum <gloss>The manuscript uses *al-hall*, likely referring to the point of motion or release.</gloss> moves. Since the length of the forceps is much greater than the root of the tooth—the point upon which it moves—by a great deal <gloss>2. The manuscript reads *hiya kabir*; the translator suggests "a large amount" (*shay'an kabiran*)</gloss>, this allows the hand <gloss>3. The word "hand" (*al-yad*) is added for clarity here</gloss> to prevail over the force residing in the root of the tooth. This is because there is no difference between the movement of a weight and the movement of a force that balances that weight.

<summary>This page explains the mechanical advantage of using forceps for tooth extraction, illustrating how the length of the tool's handles acts as a lever to overcome the resistance of the tooth's root.</summary>
<keywords>leverage, forceps (al-kallabatayn), molar extraction, Aristotelian mechanics, fulcrum, mechanical advantage, dental tools</keywords>

...some parts overlap with others, encountering only a small amount of air resistance. However, a weight that descends crosswise has many parts, and each part possesses power proportional to its width during the movement of this weight. Every part receives a portion of the driving force in proportion to its specific weight, rather than the entire object being moved by a single, unified force. ۝

**6** Why does shooting from the middle of the bowstring send the arrow a much greater distance? <gloss>1. The manuscript reads "after" [ba'da] instead of "distance" [bu'dan]</gloss>
This is because the tension is greatest at the center, and the propelling force is maximized. For this reason, bows are constructed from horn to allow for flexibility; when the bow is deeply bent, the string acts upon the arrow with more tension, creating a greater force that allows it to travel further. Conversely, stiff bows whose ends do not yield to bending send the arrow a shorter distance. ۝

**7** Why does wood break more easily when the "knee" <note>The "knee" [al-rukba] refers to the point of pressure or the fulcrum where the wood is being bent or snapped.</note> is positioned exactly in the middle?
This is because if the "knee" is placed anywhere other than the center, one side becomes longer than the other. It then functions like a scale divided into two unequal sections. The hand <gloss>2. We add the word "hand" [al-yad] here for clarity</gloss> that is further from the "knee" exerts more power than the hand closer to it. One hand does not gain an advantage over the other unless both are positioned at the very ends of the wood. ۝

**8** Why does a piece of wood become weaker and bend more as its length increases, even when supported at one of its ends? This is because in long wood...

**3** Why does the same weight added to balanced scales produce a different **<term>inclination [mayl]</term>** <note>The "inclination" refers to the downward tendency or the degree of tilt in the scale beam.</note>? Its effect—the greatest inclination—occurs when the initial weight is smaller. For if there were two scale pans, each containing three **<term>minas</term>** <note>The "mina" (plural "umna") was an ancient unit of weight used throughout the Near East and Greece.</note>, and we placed half a mina in one of the pans, that pan would tilt significantly. However, if there were ten minas in each pan and we added half a mina to one of the pans, the movement of the beam would be very slight. This occurs because the weight moves according to the proportion of the force; for the three minas are moved by [an additional] one-sixth, whereas the ten minas are moved by [an additional] one-twentieth <note>The text literally says "half of a tenth," which equals 1/20.</note>. This is because half a mina is one-twentieth of ten minas, but it is one-sixth of three minas. A weight that is moved by a greater proportional force moves more easily. ۝

**4** Why do heavy weights descend to the earth in less time than those that are lighter? Just as it happens that an object moves more easily if the external **<term>moving force</term>** <note>In this context, "moving force" [al-quwwa al-muharrika] refers to an applied push or pull.</note> is greater, so it is when its **internal power** <margin>1. "Internal power" [nafsiha] is a conjecture; the original word is worn away in the manuscript.</margin> is greater—it moves more easily. The power and the **<term>attraction [al-mujdhub]</term>** <note>This refers to the inherent pull or gravity within the object itself.</note> in a larger weight during **<term>natural movements</term>** <note>Aristotelian physics defines "natural movement" as the motion of an element toward its natural place, such as heavy earth falling downward.</note> are greater than they are in a smaller weight. ۝

**5** Why does a single weight, if it is wide, descend to the earth more slowly than if it were round? It is not, as some people have thought, simply because the flat object encounters more air due to its width. Rather, as for the round object, its parts...

<meta>This page corresponds to folio 80 of the manuscript. Although the previous page provided in the prompt was 81 (discussing scales and falling weights), the content here continues the exploration of mechanical principles found in the Arabic tradition of the *Problemata Mechanica*. This page specifically concludes a discussion on collective labor and addresses the physics of carts and terrain resistance.</meta>

->٨٠<-

...movement between the movement of an individual and the movement of a group. But we see that movement is easier for a group because, in a group, each individual carries a portion of the load, making its movement easier for them. It is evident to us that the load is divided among those who move it. ⁂

**[٣٤] A** Why are carts with two **<term>wheels [falaka]</term>** <note>The Arabic term *falaka* literally means a whorl or a disk-like object, here referring to wheels.</note> easier for carrying heavy loads than four-wheeled carts? This is perhaps because the weight in a two-wheeled cart is divided into two equal parts on either side of the **<term>axle [mihwar]</term>**. However, in a four-wheeled cart, that balance is not easily achieved and the weight is not so divided; thus, its two parts¹ on both sides are not equal. Instead, the entire load sits in front of the two rear wheels and behind the two front wheels. This difference in the placement of the weight hinders the speed of the wheel's movement, for a wheel only moves swiftly when its weight is equal in all its parts. ⁂

**B** Why does pulling a cart become difficult for beasts of burden in the sand? It is because some of the **<term>spokes [hawks]</term>** <note>The term *hawks* is obscure in the Arabic text; it is likely a transliteration of a Greek technical term, possibly referring to the spokes or the hub of the wheel.</note> of the wheel sink into the depths of the sand. When the wheel is pulled, the sand in front of it resists and pushes against the wheel. Furthermore, it is difficult because the legs of the animals sink into the sand, making it hard to pull them out; whereas on solid ground, this does not occur. ⁂

<meta>This page (79) contains the prologue to the treatise, establishing the philosophical principles of mechanics. It flows directly into the text provided in the previous page translation (80), which discusses the distribution of weight among a group of movers.</meta>

->٧٩<-

...its causes except after visible things, so our wonder at that increases when we see things that we use acting contrary to what we are accustomed to and what we have known. It is apparent to us that it is strictly necessary for anyone who wishes to investigate thoroughly the existence of causes to employ natural principles—whether one or many—and to refer everything asked <gloss>1: The manuscript reads "he asks" for "whenever he asks"</gloss> about back to them. He should resolve every one of the problems through thorough investigation once its cause is revealed and found to be within the thing we have already recognized. Let it be a premise for us that the light is easy to move and the heavy is difficult to move, and that the movement of a single weight by a greater force is easier than by a lesser force; for we see this as it is, and it is clear and apparent to us. 

We must also know that everything we ask about may contain something hidden that is not apparent, because one hardly asks <gloss>2: The manuscript reads "he asks"</gloss> about something whose cause is apparent and clear. However, we must know that the beginning of all problems that occur in the **<term>art of mechanics [ṣināʿat al-ḥiyal]</term>** <note>Literally "the art of ingenious devices" or "stratagems." In the medieval Arabic tradition, this refers to the science of machines, such as levers, pulleys, and windlasses, which allow small forces to move large weights.</note> and the hiddenness of the cause therein, is that we cannot see heavy bodies as they are divided among the forces moving them. This cause is apparent in many things, especially in the movers of these bodies; for a body that a single man cannot move, or which is difficult for him to move alone, a group of men can move, and its movement is easy for them. If it were the case that the weight of the entire moved object fell upon each one of the movers, there would be no difference...

<summary>This page introduces the theoretical foundation of mechanics, explaining that "ingenious devices" create wonder by using hidden natural principles to overcome heavy weights. It establishes the basic premise that force can be divided among multiple movers to make a task easier.</summary>

<keywords>mechanics, causes, motion, weight, force, ingenious devices, collective labor, natural principles</keywords>

<meta>This page (78) continues the technical discussion of mechanical forces. While the previous page (79) established the philosophical necessity of natural principles in mechanics, this page transitions into the practical application of these principles, specifically addressing the discrepancy between theoretical mathematical ratios and the physical reality of friction and machine construction.</meta>

->( 78 )<-

**[32]** Because we have explained, for each one of these powers, that it is possible for a known force to move a known weight, we should also understand the following: if it were possible for all components to be precisely turned on a lathe, equal in weight, uniform in their parts, and perfectly smooth, then we could apply the weights we mentioned in each of these machines according to those exact ratios. However, since it is not possible for people to achieve <gloss>1: The manuscript reads "they know" [yaʿlamūn], but the context suggests "they do" or "they make" [yaʿmalūn]</gloss> such perfection in smoothness and evenness, the applied forces must be increased to compensate for the roughness occurring in the machines. We must increase the force by a certain amount beyond the ratios we previously established, so that we do not encounter resistance. Our practical application of these machines must not contradict what has been proven true by logical demonstration.

**[33]** It is strictly necessary for those who wish to master the **<term>art of mechanics [ṣināʿat al-ḥiyal]</term>** <note>Literally "the art of ingenious devices." In the medieval Arabic tradition, this refers to the science of engineering and machines—such as pulleys, levers, and windlasses—which allow a small force to overcome a great weight.</note> to understand the causes that arise in every movement, just as we have explained regarding the lifting of heavy objects through natural demonstrations. We have provided an account of everything that occurs for each of the powers we mentioned, so that nothing happens to them <gloss>2: The manuscript uses an alternative spelling for "something" [shay'an]</gloss> without a proof, or results in something they doubt. Rather, when they investigate any of their requirements, the truth of what we have mentioned will be revealed in every instance. Let us now mention certain things discussed by the ancients <note>This likely refers to Greek authorities such as Archimedes, Hero of Alexandria, or Pappus, whose works formed the basis of Arabic mechanics.</note> that are useful in this field. We may wonder at some of these things; for once they are explained, they may seem contrary to our previous understanding. Our inquiry shall begin with what is apparent to us and what we are currently unable to explain. ۞

<meta>This page (77) continues the technical analysis of simple machines from the previous page (78). ->( 77 )<-

Mark H with a line at right angles, which is line H-C <gloss>1: The manuscript reads "line" [khaṭṭāh] incorrectly here</gloss>. Let a **<term>wedge [asfīn]</term>** <note>A triangular tool used to split objects or lift loads; one of the six classical simple machines.</note> be prepared in the shape of a rhombus, specifically A-B-D-H. Let its side, which is B-D, enter so that a small part of it lies under the load, and let its head be A-H. It will then appear to us that when the wedge A-B-C is struck, it penetrates A-B-D-H. 

The proof of this is that if we extend lines A-B and D-H to point Z, an angle is formed equal to angle A-B-C. Thus, A-Z-H also becomes a wedge <gloss>2: wedge</gloss> that can be moved by that same force. Let us imagine the parts marked B-Z-D to be under the load; thus, the wedge has been prepared. This is the explanation of the wedge. 

It is not strictly necessary to use an acute angle for the wedge, because we have demonstrated that every slight strike can move any wedge if it is struck many times. Our use of small angles is intended only for small strikes. Therefore, it is not strictly necessary to use small angles for the wedge.

**[31]** As for the **<term>screw [al-lawlab]</term>** <note>Used here to describe a threaded mechanism, likely for a press or a lifting device.</note>, it is possible <gloss>3: The manuscript incorrectly reads "it is not possible"</gloss> to use a similar process. For this, we need to set a perpendicular line A-C upon B-C at the angle of the helical circle (which is angle A-B-C), equal to the thickness of the **<term>tholos [al-ṭūlus]</term>** <note>Derived from the Greek word for a circular building or dome; in this mechanical context, it refers to the cylindrical core or a specific fitting part of the screw assembly.</note> that we wish to fit into the screw-groove. 

We then construct a cylinder whose circumference is equal to line Z-H <gloss>4: The manuscript reads B-C</gloss>. We draw a helical circle from these lines at the distance A-H <gloss>5: The manuscript reads A-C</gloss>. We carve the helical circle, and its distance will be equal to line A-C. Through this process, we can fit that piece of wood into the screw-groove.

<meta>This page follows the discussion of mechanical components—specifically the screw and axle mechanism—by describing how various simple machines are combined to move a massive weight. The previous page concluded with a description of the "chest-like" frame; this page provides the geometric arrangement of the lever and pulleys that connect to that frame.</meta>

->—+( 15 )+—<-

The known weight [can be moved] by the combination and overlapping of these components with one another, except for the **wedge**; for it alone does not move anything except by a strike <note>Original: *bi-al-ḍarbah*. Heron distinguishes the wedge from other simple machines because it requires impact (kinetic energy) rather than steady force (static pressure).</note>. Let us now demonstrate that it is possible for us to move the known weight by combining the four powers. 

Let the known weight be at mark **A**. Let there be a **lever** <term>lever [maḥall]: a rigid bar used to exert pressure.</term> at marks **B** and **C**. Let mark **B**, which is the end of the lever, be placed under the **fulcrum** <note>Arabic: *maḥmal*, literally 'the carrier' or support.</note>, and let mark **C** be elevated. Let the **pivot** <note>Arabic: *maḥjar*. In this context, the point upon which the lever turns.</note> on which the lever moves be mark **D**. Let the distance **CD** be five times the distance **DB**. Therefore, the force required at **C** would be two hundred **quintals** <term>quintal [qanṭār]: a traditional unit of mass used here to illustrate weight ratios.</term> to balance the weight at **A** <note>This implies the total weight at A is 1,000 quintals, following the 1:5 ratio of the lever arms.</note>. 

Let us fasten to the end of the lever at mark **C** a large lifting machine at mark **E**. Let the other machine be parallel to it in a fixed corner at mark **Z**. Let the part that pulls this machine be at mark **G**, and let it have five **pulleys** <note>Arabic: *bakar*. The mechanical advantage increases with the number of pulleys in the system.</note>. Thus, the pulling force becomes forty quintals. 

Let there be an **axle** on a **wheel** marked **T K**. As for the axle, it is at mark **T**, and as for the wheel, it is at mark **K**. Let the rope that runs over the pulley be wound around the axle. Let the wheel have teeth standing upon the surface [of its edge], and let a **screw** be fitted into its teeth—this is screw **L**. Let it have handles to turn it at mark **M**, and let the teeth be fitted into the screw's grooves. When we wish to turn the screw, the wheel **K** turns by the rotation of the screw; this rotation then turns axle **T**, and the rope belonging to the pulleys winds around it, thereby pressing down the end of the lever at **C**.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mechanical ratios, specifically transitioning from the combined power of levers and pulleys (the "four powers") to the specific properties of the wedge and the screw. It reinforces the fundamental mechanical principle that a reduction in required force results in a corresponding increase in the time required to complete the work.</meta>

->Page 74<-

The circles of the axles are the circles drawn by marks **B**, **Q**, and **T**, and the wheel refers to the circles drawn by marks **J**, **Z**, and **K**. Just as we demonstrated regarding those axles—that the ratio of force to force is as the ratio of time to time—the same is demonstrated in this case as well. ۝ <note>This symbol is a decorative separator used in the manuscript to mark the end of a section.</note>

**[27]** Regarding the **wedge** <term>wedge [isfīn]: a triangular tool used to split or lift objects through impact.</term> and the **screw** <term>screw [lawlab]: a spiral-threaded component used to apply high pressure.</term>, we cannot say this. As we have explained before, no resistance occurs in them; rather, the opposite happens. Whenever the force in them increases, each of them becomes smaller. Our objective was merely to find a way to manage what increases in magnitude—such as an increase in **weight**—so that we can work on it with small tools to make [the task] easier <note>The manuscript originally read *fasahal* ("and it was easy"), but the corrected reading is *fasahula* ("so it becomes easy").</note>. Therefore, for the wedge and the screw, we do not need to find a way to make them smaller to facilitate the work. ۝

**[28]** As for the fact that slowness <note>Arabic: *ibṭā’*. In classical mechanics, this refers to the trade-off where a mechanical advantage results in a slower movement or a longer duration of effort.</note> also occurs with these two, that is evident. This is because many strikes take more **time** than a single strike, and many rotations of the screw take more time than a single rotation. We have already demonstrated that the ratio of the wedge's angle to the [other] angle is as the ratio of the moving strike to the [other] moving strike. Thus, the ratio of time is as the ratio of **force** to force. ۝

**[29]** Regarding what has preceded, we have moved the known weight using many axles within a **wheel** <note>Arabic: *falak*. The **lever** <term>lever [al-maḥall]: a rigid bar used with a fulcrum to move a heavy load with less effort.</term> is at mark **D**. Let the movement of the weight be by the lever, while it is parallel to the ground. Let **GD** be five times **DB**; thus, the force at **G** which balances the one thousand **quintals** <term>quintal [qunṭār]: a traditional unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 100 pounds or a hundredweight.</term> will be two hundred quintals.

Let there be another lever, which is **HZ**. Let mark **E**, which is at the head of the lever, be connected to mark **G**, so that by the movement of **E**, **G** is moved. Let the **axle** <term>axle [miḥwar]: here referring to the fulcrum or the rotating shaft supporting the lever.</term> which is under the lever be at mark **H**, and let it be movable toward **D**. Let **ZH** be five times **HE**; thus, the force at **Z** will be forty quintals.

Let there be another lever, which is **TK**. Let us connect mark **T** to mark **Z**, and let it move in a direction opposite to the movement of **E**. Let the axle which is under the lever be at mark **L**. Let **KL** be eight times **LT**; thus, the force at **K** will be five quintals, and it will balance the weight. If we wish for the force to overcome the weight, we must make **KL** greater than eight times **LT**. If **KL** is eight times **LT**, and **ZH** is five times **HE**, and **GD** is more than five times **DB**, then the force will overcome the weight. ۝ <note>This symbol marks the conclusion of the mathematical proof for the triple-lever system.</note>

->٦٨<-

[٢١] We state that the circle is the [most efficient] of the shapes related to it—namely, **spheres** <term>spheres [ukar]: solid round objects.</term> and **cylinders** <term>cylinders [asāṭīn]: rollers or drum-shaped components.</term>—for their motion is circular, as we demonstrated in the book preceding this one. <note>Refers to Book I of Heron’s Mechanics, which covers theoretical geometry and the properties of circles.</note> Our objective is to first move a massive weight by means of an **axle** <term>axle [miḥwar]: the central shaft on which a wheel or gear rotates.</term> set within a **pulley** <term>pulley [falka]: here referring to a large wheel or circular drum used to gain mechanical advantage.</term> using only a small force, without encountering resistance.

Let the weight we wish to move be, for example, one thousand **quintals** <term>quintal [qinṭār]: a traditional unit of weight, roughly 100 lbs.</term>, and let the force we use to move it be five quintals. First, we must make the force equal to the weight, for once this equilibrium is achieved, we can then make that force overcome the weight by adding a slight amount of extra effort to the machine.

Let us designate the axle around which the **rope** <term>rope [qalas]: a heavy lifting cable.</term> tied to the weight is wound with the mark **A**. Let the pulley mounted upon it be mark **B**. To simplify the construction of the machine, let the diameter of pulley B be five times the diameter of the axle. In this case, the force required at pulley B to balance a weight of one thousand quintals would be two hundred quintals. <note>Because of the 5:1 ratio, the force needed is 1/5th of the total weight: 1,000 / 5 = 200.</note> 

However, our available force is only five quintals. Therefore, we cannot move the specified weight using pulley B alone with this force. Let us then introduce a **toothed axle** <term>toothed axle [miḥwar muḍarras]: an axle equipped with gear teeth or cogs.</term>, which is axle **C**, engaged with the **teeth** <margin>original: "aḍrās" — cogs or gear teeth</margin> of pulley B. Thus, when axle C moves, its motion will turn pulley B along with the primary axle. Consequently, when axle C is moved, the weight will be moved. This axle will be moved by the force that moves pulley B, because we have already proven that for any two circles situated on their own centers... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the geometric proof on the next page.</note>

<meta>This page (Page 97) follows the introduction of the first stage of mechanical reduction. While the previous page calculated a 5:1 ratio to bring a 1,000-quintal load down to a 200-quintal requirement, this page addresses the physical limitations of using a single simple machine for the entire reduction and introduces the necessity of compounding these machines.</meta>

->97<-

A certain impossibility of action may arise when we desire to move massive **weights** <term>weights [athqāl]: the heavy loads intended to be moved.</term> with a very small force. Regarding the first three [simple machines], a problem occurs because their size must be increased in proportion to the increase of the weight we wish to lift. I am referring here to the **wheel** <term>wheel [falka]: a large circular drum or pulley-wheel.</term> that is mounted upon the **axle** <term>axle [miḥwar]: the central shaft around which the rope or wheel turns.</term>, the **lever** <term>lever [maḥal]: a rigid bar used with a pivot to gain mechanical advantage.</term>, and the machine known as the **Great Lifter**. <note>original: "al-ālah al-latī tusammā kabīrat al-raf‘"; this likely refers to a compound pulley system or a complex gear train described by Heron.</note>

As for the remaining two—namely, the one that operates by the **screw** <term>screw [lawlab]: a threaded cylinder used to convert rotational motion into linear force.</term>—a different problem occurs, as we would need to decrease its size by that same proportion. 

For example, if we wished to move a weight of one thousand **quintals** <term>quintal [qinṭār]: a unit of weight, roughly 100 lbs or 45 kg.</term> using a force equal to only five quintals, and we employed the wheel-and-axle for this movement, the line extending from the center of the wheel to its outer edge <note>The radius of the wheel.</note> would need to be two hundred times longer than the line extending from the center of the axle to its outer edge <note>The radius of the axle.</note>—and even slightly more than that. <note>1. ms. "slightly" [qalīlan]</note>

If we were to use the lever for this, we would need the longer section—the part closest to the force moving the weight—to be in this same ratio [200:1], or slightly more. Using such machines in this manner is difficult, or rather, nearly impossible. For if we made the diameter of the axle half a **cubit** <term>cubit [dhirā‘]: an ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger.</term> so that it would be strong enough for the load to be suspended from it, we would then be required to make the diameter of the wheel one hundred cubits or more. Such a thing is difficult to construct. 

The same difficulty arises with the lever and with the Great Lifter, for we cannot divide the lever into such proportions, nor can we use such a vast number of **pulleys**. <term>pulleys [bakar]: grooved wheels used with ropes to change the direction or magnitude of a force.</term> Let us now, therefore, devise a contrivance to ease the hindrance that occurs with these three mechanical powers.

<summary>Heron explains that using a single machine (like a wheel-and-axle or lever) to move a 1,000-quintal weight with a 5-quintal force is impractical because it would require impossibly large components, such as a 100-cubit wheel.</summary>

<meta>This page (Page 66) continues the technical analysis of the fifth simple machine—the screw—and concludes the general overview of the "Five Powers" of mechanics. It follows the discussion on Page 97 regarding the challenges of moving massive weights with small forces and the mathematical proportions required for mechanical advantage.</meta>

->( 66 )<-

The rod may resist the grooves of the **screw** <term>screw [lawlab]: a threaded cylinder used to convert rotational motion into linear force.</term> if its groove is carved almost parallel to the side of the **cylinder** <term>cylinder [usṭuwāna]: the solid body or shaft upon which the screw threads are cut.</term>. If there is no spiral groove on the cylinder, but instead a channel-like groove along one of its sides, then the rod known as the **pin** <term>pin [ṭūlus]: derived from the Greek word 'tylos,' referring to a peg or tooth that engages with the threads of a screw.</term> will offer strong resistance to this channel-like groove.

When the spiral threads are close together and we lift the piece of wood called the pin, the **weight** <term>weight [thiql]: the heavy load being moved.</term> will not move unless there is a great force to move the pin. However, if the weight is suspended from the pin and the spiral threads are close together, the weight rises when we turn the screw. If we stop turning the screw, the weight comes to rest and remains suspended. <note>This describes the "self-locking" property of a fine-threaded screw, where friction prevents the load from back-driving the mechanism.</note>

If the spiral threads are upright <note>Meaning a very steep pitch.</note>, we cannot move the weight unless there is a great force that overcomes the screw. We have now spoken regarding the nature of the screw and provided a sufficient summary of it.

[20] As for the five powers that move a weight having an analogy to circles sharing a single center, this has been clarified in the previous figures we have drawn. We see<gloss>we see [ms: I see]</gloss> that they are closer in analogy to the **balance** <term>balance [mīzān]: a scale used to compare weights; the primary model for understanding the lever and mechanical advantage.</term> than to circles. This is because, as previously mentioned, the primary proofs regarding circles were derived for us through the balance. 

It is also shown<gloss>shown [conjectural reading due to manuscript damage]</gloss> that the ratio of the weight suspended on the smaller side to that suspended on the larger side is as the ratio of the larger part of the balance arm to the smaller part. This principle applies to all five powers. <note>This is the Law of the Lever: force multiplied by the length of the lever arm equals the load multiplied by the length of the load arm.</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the screw, specifically exploring the mechanics of its "pitch"—how the distance between the spiral grooves determines whether the machine can hold a weight in place or allow it to slide back. It expands on the relationship between the screw and the 'pin' (tylos) introduced on the previous page.</meta>

->( 95 )<-

...engineered onto the **groove** <term>groove [maḥfar]: the recessed path carved into the cylinder to form the screw thread.</term> and it was like a **spade** <note>The author uses 'spade' or 'shovel' (masḥāh) as a mechanical analogy for the tooth or engagement point that fits into the screw's threads.</note>. Thus, we need to make the circles of the **screw** <term>screw [lawlab]: a spiral thread wrapped around a cylinder.</term> close together so they are almost parallel to the base of the **cylinder** <term>cylinder [usṭuwāna]: the central shaft of the screw.</term> upon which the screw is drawn. For if the circles are arranged this way, they resemble a spade—acting as the shaft that transfers the **weight** <term>weight [thiql]: the heavy load to be moved or supported.</term>.

However, if the spiral circles in the screw-groove are very steep <note>Literally "upright," referring to a high-pitched screw where the threads wrap vertically rather than horizontally.</note>, such that they are almost parallel to the length of the cylinder, then the curved rod called the **pin** <term>pin [ṭūlus]: a term derived from the Greek *tylos*, referring to the peg that engages with the screw's threads.</term>, if a heavy load is hung from it or a great force is applied to it, will, upon the turning of the screw, quickly turn in the opposite direction to the first. 

From this, it becomes clear to us that the screw is capable of moving the rod called the **pin**, and it can also be moved by this rod. It moves the rod if the screw-groove has circles that are close together; in this case, when the turning of the screw ceases, it stays in its place and the load remains suspended upon it. <note>This describes the principle of "mechanical advantage" and "self-locking," where friction in a fine thread prevents a heavy weight from falling back down.</note>

But if the screw-groove is very steep, and it does not stay still when the turning of the screw ceases, then it is the rod that moves the screw. This is because if a cord is tied within the carved groove of the screw, and a weight is tied to the end of that cord, and the screw-groove is very steep, we say that if we lift the rod called the **pin**, the weight is also lifted. If we stop lifting the rod, the weight comes to rest and remains suspended because this...

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how a screw engages with a gear or a "tylos" (pin). The previous page established that the pitch of the screw—whether the threads are close together or steep—determines the mechanical advantage and whether the weight can be held in place. This page provides a geometric demonstration of the screw's rotation relative to the pegs on a wheel.</meta>

->( 213 )<-

Regarding the **spiral circles** <term>spiral circles [al-dawā’ir al-lawlabiyya]: the continuous threads wrapped around the cylinder of a screw.</term>, each one of them is a single circle. Let us imagine a **wheel** <term>wheel [falka]: a rotating disc or gear designed to interact with the screw.</term> is placed, fitted with **pegs** <term>pegs [awtād]: pins or teeth protruding from the wheel to catch the screw's thread.</term> marked H, H, H, H. Let its pegs be J, J, J, J, and let them be fitted into the spiral circles. Let the assembly be perfectly fitted at the point where it enters a spiral circle. The other pegs will not yet be engaged in the other spiral circles. 

If we then turn the **screw** <term>screw [lawlab]: the threaded cylinder that drives the mechanism.</term> until the mark A is pushed toward J, A will arrive at J. When the screw completes one full rotation, and peg J has moved into the position of the [previous] peg J, peg H will also have moved into the position of the [previous] peg H. This is because in one single rotation of the screw, it moves the distance of exactly one peg-interval. One should imagine the same for the other pegs: the screw must make as many rotations as there are pegs on the wheel for the wheel to complete one full revolution.

When the screw rotates, it moves the wooden piece called the **tylos** <term>tylos [ṭūlus]: a term transliterated from Greek into Arabic, referring to a pin or bolt that follows the screw's thread.</term> according to our previous description, and it lifts the **weight** <term>weight [thiql]: the heavy load or resistance being moved by the machine.</term> in a straight line. It is necessary that this tylos, when the screw is not moving, remains steady and fixed <note>The marginal note indicates a variation in the manuscript: "steady and fixed" [hādī thābit].</note> in its place by whatever force it possesses. When the screw stops turning, the pressure of the weight must not overcome it. By this I mean that this rod must be fitted into the **screw-groove** <term>screw-groove [al-ḥufr al-lawlabī]: the recessed path or "valley" between the threads of the screw.</term> and everything that acts as a support for it, so that it does not slip out of the screw-groove. For if it slips, the entire weight will drop back down to the position from which it was lifted. This rod does not slip from the screw-groove if the end of the rod...

<summary>This page describes the geometric relationship between a screw's rotation and the movement of a pegged wheel (a worm gear mechanism), explaining that one rotation of the screw advances the wheel by one peg. It also stresses the importance of a secure fit between the screw and the 'tylos' (pin) to prevent the weight from slipping backward.</summary>

# Page 63

...**tylos** <term>tylos [ṭūlus]: a Greek-derived term for a bolt or pin that engages with the screw's thread.</term>. For this reason, the **screw** <term>screw [lawlab]: here described as a "wound-up wedge."</term> has become a wound-up wedge that meets [it] <gloss>¹ "not"</gloss> <note>original Arabic: "لا" (not). The French editors note they added this word to make sense of the sentence.</note> acting by a blow; rather, through its rotation and turning, it performs the function of a blow, and thus the **weight** <term>weight [thiql]: the load being moved or lifted by the machine.</term> is lightened. 

Its reduction of the load is the opposite of the action performed by the **wedge** <term>wedge [asfīn]: a triangular tool used to split objects or lift weights.</term>. This is because the wedge acts by penetrating inward; it moves the weight while the weight remains fixed in its place. As for the screw, it is a wound wedge that remains fixed in its place, and the weight is moved toward it. 

Just as it has been demonstrated regarding the wedge that the one with a smaller angle moves the weight with less force than the force required to move the weight with a wedge having a larger angle, the same must be said of this screw: those in which the distances between the **spiral threads** <term>spiral threads [al-dawā’ir al-lawlabiyya]: the helical paths wrapped around the cylinder.</term> are smaller move the weight with greater ease than the movement of a screw where the distances between its spiral threads are larger. This is because the smallness of the distance makes the angles smaller. Thus, the screw <gloss>²</gloss> <note>The French editors note that the Arabic word for screw (*lawlab*) is used as both masculine and feminine in this sentence.</note> whose threads are steeper <note>Referring to a high-pitch screw where the threads are more vertical.</note> moves the weight with greater force, and that which is lower <note>Referring to a low-pitch screw with "flatter" or more horizontal threads.</note> moves the weight with less force.

...the **hypotenuse** <term>hypotenuse [watar]: the longest side of a right-angled triangle, opposite the right angle.</term> of the right angle. We make a triangle from **thin brass** <term>thin brass [ṣufr raqīq]: a flexible sheet of metal used here as a template for drawing geometric curves.</term> equal to this triangle, ensuring its thickness is such that we can bend it as we wish. Once we have done this, we align the side—the one equal to the width of the **spiral thread’s** <term>spiral thread [al-dā’ira al-lawlabiyya]: the helical path that winds around the cylinder.</term> position—with the first of the equal divisions we marked along the side of the **cylinder** <term>cylinder [al-usṭuwāna]: the wooden or metal shaft that forms the core of the screw.</term>. 

Then, we wrap the thin brass triangle around the cylindrical wood. The remaining acute angle of the triangle will meet the right angle of the brass sheet because the base of the triangle is equal to the circumference of the cylinder. We then join both angles and trace the spiral thread along the hypotenuse of the right angle. Next, we shift the triangle to the second division and align the side of the thin triangle with that second section. Following the same procedure as the first, we trace the second spiral thread so it is adjacent to the first. We continue this until we have traced all the divisions of the cylindrical wood. 

This is necessary because, when we operate the screw, we need to place [a component] into the first groove of the wood’s spiral thread. This component is called the **tylos** <term>tylos [ṭūlus]: a Greek-derived term for a pin, bolt, or tooth that engages with the screw's thread to transmit motion.</term>, and it is what supports the weight. When the screw is rotated, this rod rises, and by its rising, the weight is lifted. ۞

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mechanical advantage, transitioning from the physics of wedges—where a smaller angle allows a smaller force to achieve the same result over more time—to the practical geometric construction of a screw thread on a cylindrical or conical body.</meta>

Regarding each of the remaining **wedges** <term>wedge [isfīn]: a triangular tool used to split or lift objects by converting a downward force into a lateral one.</term>, if the object being moved is driven by just one of the smaller wedges, then after many blows and pushes, it will move a distance equal to what the entire large wedge moves in a single strike. This motion is achieved by a series of blows—specifically, the number of strikes labeled B, J, H, T, and TJ <note>These letters refer to points or quantities in a geometric diagram common in Greek and Arabic mechanics.</note>. On this basis, the ratio of time to time is as the ratio of strikes to strikes. Furthermore, the ratio of the large wedge's head to the head of one of the small wedges is proportional. Thus, to the degree that the angle of the wedge is made smaller, the wedge penetrates with a smaller amount of force than the force required to drive the entire wedge <note>original: "wa-tanfudhu" - the manuscript contains a slight variation in the spelling of the verb for "penetrate".</note>.

**[16]** It remains now for us to explain the underlying principle of the **screw** <term>screw [al-lawlab]: a mechanical device that converts rotational motion into linear motion, traditionally analyzed in mechanics as a wedge wrapped around a cylinder.</term>. Let us begin first by establishing the requirements for drawing the **spiral threads** <term>spiral threads [al-dawā’ir al-lawlabiyya]: the helical path wound around the core of the screw.</term>. We say that if we wish to draw a screw, we must take a strong, hard rod of the required length. Let the object we wish to **fashion into a screw** <note>The author uses the noun "screw" (lawlab) here as a transitive verb, meaning to carve or shape a rod into a threaded screw.</note> be shaped as a **cone** <term>cone [makhrūṭ]: a solid object that tapers from a circular base to a point.</term>, or let its thickness be uniform so that its surface forms a **cylinder** <term>cylinder [al-usṭuwāna]: a solid with two parallel circular bases.</term>. We then divide this side into equal parts corresponding to the width of the spiral thread. On its surface, we assume two straight lines, one perpendicular to the other. We then connect the ends of these two lines with a single line...

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric demonstration of how a large wedge can be subdivided into smaller, identical wedges. It establishes the proportional relationship between the force applied (the "strike"), the distance of penetration, and the time required, proving that the mechanical advantage remains consistent whether the force is applied in one large blow or several smaller ones.</meta>

...are equal. If we connect points **F**, **Q**, and **R** to point **A**, four triangles are formed with their vertices at point **A** and their bases being the lines **MF**, **FQ**, **QR**, and **RD**. Each of these moves by a force <term>force [ḍarba]: literally "a strike" or "blow," referring to the impulse or power applied to the tool</term> equal to the force **BE**, traversing a distance subtracted from line **AD** <note>original: "ād"</note>. 

Thus, it is the same as saying that force **BE** drives through the entire wedge <term>wedge [al-isfīn]: a triangular mechanical tool used to split or lift</term> for the distance **DR**—which is to say, distance **KA**—and that force **BE** drives through the wedge whose head is **RD** for the distance **AD**. For by the motion of the entire wedge, line **KA** moves by the distance **AK** <note>original: "ak"</note>; and by the motion of the wedge whose head is **RD**, the distance <note>original: "wa-al-bu‘d" (the distance)</note> equal to line **KA**—which is the distance **RD**—moves by the distance **AD**. 

Thus, **RD** moves by force **BE** for the distance **AD**. From this, it becomes clear to us that the ratio of force **BE** to the total force **BJ** is the same as the ratio of the wedge whose head is **RD** to the entire wedge. 

Likewise, this applies to the amount of time in which the wedge with head line **RD** moves, as well as the amount of distance the entire wedge moves under force **BJ**; the ratio is the same as the ratio of force **BE** to the total force <term>total force [al-ḍarba al-kulliya]: the cumulative power or the sum of all individual strikes</term>. 

From another perspective, we find no difference between the movement of force **BJ** on the head **DM**—meaning the entire wedge—and the movement of each individual force **BE**, **QH**, **HT**, and **TJ** upon each of the wedges whose heads are **MF**, **FQ**, **QR**, and **RD**. This is because the partial forces together equal the total force. Thus, force **BE** drives through the wedge with head **MF** to the same degree that the total force drives through the entire wedge, as do each of the remaining forces.

<summary>This page concludes a geometric proof showing that the mechanical work performed by a single large force on a wedge is equivalent to the sum of smaller forces applied to its components. It establishes that the ratio of partial force to total force is proportional to the ratio of the wedge's segments to its whole.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, mechanics, wedge, proportionality, force, ratio, triangles, Heron of Alexandria</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric demonstration of the wedge's mechanics. The previous page established that the ratio of a partial strike to a total strike is proportional to the ratio of the smaller wedge segment to the whole. This page moves into the proof that even the smallest conceivable force results in a measurable displacement, further breaking down the wedge into parallel sections to illustrate the relationship between force, distance, and the tool's geometry.</meta>

It appears to us that the <term>strike [ḍarba]: a blow or application of force</term> does not remain upon the <term>wedge [al-isfīn]: a triangular mechanical tool</term> for even the shortest duration of time; rather, the wedge takes on a motion immediately after the strike.

[15] Therefore, I say that every strike, even if it is slight, moves the entire wedge. Let us assume a certain wedge whose vertex is at point **A** and whose <term>head [ra's]: the wide base of the wedge</term> is the line **DM**. Let its movement be caused by strike **BJ**, and its length <note>the distance of penetration</note> be **AD**, and let it be assumed that it can be moved by a slight strike. Let us separate from the total strike **BJ** a smaller strike, which is strike **BE**, being smaller than all known strikes <note>original: "al-ma‘lūma"</note>. I say that the strike **BE**, by its own nature, pushes some part of the wedge.

The proof of this is as follows: because strike **BJ** moves [the wedge] through the distance **AD**, then [a portion of] **BJ** moves it a distance less than **AD**. Let it move the distance **AZ**. Furthermore, if the strike **BE** is added <note>meaning repeatedly applied</note>, then the entire distance **AD** is traversed by the [total] strike **BJ**. Therefore, the strike **BE** by its own nature <note>original: "fī dhātiha"; the manuscript reads "kāna" (was) instead of "dhāt" (self/essence)</note> moves the wedge through the distance **DZ**. 

If we imagine the strike **BJ** to be divided into strikes equal to **BE**—namely **BE**, **EH**, **HT**, and **TJ**—then the distance **AD** is likewise divided into equal segments [proportional to] **DZ**, which are **AK**, **KL**, **LZ**, and **ZD**. Thus, each one of the individual strikes **BE**, **EH**, **HT**, and **TJ** moves [the wedge] through each of the distances **DZ**, **ZL**, **LK**, and **KA**. 

Now, let us imagine lines parallel to line **DM**, which is the head of the wedge; these are lines **ZN**, **LS**, and **KY**. Let there also be lines parallel to line **AD**, which are lines **FN**, **QS**, and **RY**. Thus, the lines [marking the segments] are **DR**, **RQ**, **QF**, and **FM**.

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof that any force applied to a wedge, no matter how small, results in motion. It demonstrates this by dividing a large strike and the resulting penetration distance into equal, infinitesimal increments.</summary>
<keywords>wedge, mechanics, geometry, strike, proportional motion, parallel lines, displacement</keywords>

# Page 58

Rightfully has this <term>pulley [bakara]: a grooved wheel used with a rope to lift weights</term> been called "the double-lifter" <note>Arabic: *dhā raf‘ayn*</note>. When the doubled <term>rope [qals]: a thick cable or hawser</term> is divided into two extended lengths, it may be called the "single-lifter" or the "double-lifter." From this, it becomes clear to us that the other end of the rope ought to be tied to a fixed beam, rather than to the weight intended for lifting. This is because the force exerted by that fixed support balances the motive force and assists it in moving the weight. Thus, it is shown that if one end of the rope is tied to the load itself, the load balances an equal amount of force. However, if its other end is tied to a fixed beam, the force balances double its own value in weight. Consequently, the weight is moved by a force less than the force that was initially required to move it.

[12] As for the <term>wedge [isfīn]: a triangular tool used to split materials</term>, a <term>strike [ḍarba]: a sudden application of force or blow</term> moves it over a certain duration of time, for there can be no motion without time. This strike acts only through <term>contact [mumāssa]: the physical touching of surfaces</term>, which does not persist upon the wedge for even the shortest duration of time. It appears to us from this that the wedge continues to move after the strike has departed from it. We know this from another perspective as well: for some time after the strike, the wedge exhibits <unclear>vibrations</unclear> and the pulling away of <unclear>fragments</unclear> <note>The OCR 'wa-ḥayāt wa-qal‘ min qal‘a' is likely a corruption of terms describing the residual energy or physical debris resulting from the impact</note> on its own. Furthermore, the fact that a strike acts even if it does not remain upon the wedge for the duration of its action is evident to us from the stone that is thrown and the arrows shot either by hand or by some other engine. For after the stone leaves the hand, we see it travel to a distant place by force, without the hand continuing to push it. From here...

# Page 57

Regarding the <term>rope [qals]: a thick cable or hawser</term> which carries the weight, there remains the proof of the pulley which is called "convex for lifting." From this, it becomes clear to us that it is possible to move a known weight by a known force.

[17] It may be assumed in everything called a rope fixed to a limit that it has two capacities <gloss>only</gloss>: sometimes acting as a "single-lifter" and sometimes as a "double-lifter," <note>Arabic: *dhā raf‘ wahid* and *dhā raf‘ayn*</note> according to the amount of force we employ with it. For example, let us assume a <term>pulley [bakara]: a grooved wheel</term> at mark A, upon which there is a rope. Let the two extended parts of the rope be at marks B and G.

Let B and G be tied to a certain weight, which is weight E. If we divide this weight into two halves, the two parts of the rope on either side will be balanced. This pulley is called a "single-lifter" because the force in this case is equal to the weight that balances it.

Let us also imagine another weight at mark Z, and let us tie a pulley to it, which is pulley H. We thread a rope through this pulley and fasten its two ends to a fixed <term>beam [‘āriḍa]: a horizontal supporting timber</term> so that weight Z is suspended. Each of the two extended parts of the rope would then carry <note>original: "carry" - this word was added for clarity by a later scribe</note> half of the total weight. If one of the ends of the rope tied at mark K were loosened and a person stood there to hold the rope, he would be carrying <note>original manuscript reads "holds back"; corrected to "carries"</note> only half of that weight. 

Thus, the total weight is double the force required to restrain it. It appears from this that another force—provided by the fixed beam where the end of the rope is tied—is equal to the force holding the other end. This beam also supports the weight, and therefore—

<summary>This page provides a geometric demonstration of how a pulley system (the "double-lifter") reduces the force needed to hold a weight by half. By anchoring one end of a rope to a fixed beam, the beam shares the load with the person pulling the rope.</summary>

->---( 54 )---<-

The operation whereby a slight force overcomes a great weight—this statement has indeed been voiced by those who preceded us, yet we describe it here so that our book might be complete and possess a systematic arrangement. ۞

### [μ]
<note>The Greek numeral for 40, likely marking a new chapter or proposition.</note>

Let us now speak on the underlying cause of the **<term>machine called the "great-lifter"</term>** <note>Arabic: *kabirat al-raf'*; likely referring to the Greek *Polyspaston* or a compound pulley crane</note>. Let us assume a **<term>pulley [falka]</term>** <note>A disk or circular block used in a hoisting machine</note> elevated at mark **A**, and upon it is a simple **<term>rope [qals]</term>** <note>Specifically a thick cable or hawser</note> denoted as **B-G**. A weight, **D**, is tied to the two extended ends of the rope. Let this weight be elevated above the ground. 

It then appears that the two extended parts of the **rope** are equal in their extension, and each of them bears a weight equal to half the weight of **D**. This is because if the two extended parts were not equal, the part with the greater extension would be lifted by the part that is higher up. However, we do not see any such thing occurring, because each of the two extended parts of the **rope** remains stationary. 

If we divide weight **D** into halves—meaning into two equal parts—it appears to us that the two extended parts of the **rope** will remain stationary, because the weight pulling them is the same single weight that was pulling them initially. Thus, half of the weight is equivalent to the weight equal to it. 

The two extended **parts** <note>The manuscript uses a non-standard dual form *al-jazwan*; the editor notes it should be *al-jaz'an*</note> of the **rope** are also equal from another perspective: because equal weights have been suspended from equal lines. This is because the extended **rope** touches two points, **E** and **A**, on the arc of the **pulley**.
