...to the force situated at point **B**. However, the object <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: *wa-lakinnahu*; the gloss notes the manuscript includes an extra pronoun "hu" (it/he) here</note> does not move in its entirety. This is because a portion of it is held firm by the **<term>assumed surface [al-sath al-mafrud]</term>** <note>In Greek mechanics, this refers to a theoretical plane or supporting base used to calculate equilibrium</note>. That portion is exactly one half of it. 

If we did not orient ourselves to that surface, and instead increased the moving force by an amount equal to that **<term>joint [mufsal]</term>** <note>Likely referring to the pivot point or fulcrum</note>, the force would drive downward and the end of the load at point **A** would rise <gloss>2</gloss> <note>The transcription notes the addition of the word "which" (*al-ladhi*) for grammatical clarity</note>. This occurs because weights <gloss>3</gloss> <note>The manuscript contains a scribe's error here, writing "transfer" (*al-intiqal*) instead of "load" (*al-haml*)</note> are distributed among the forces moving them in equal proportions. 

Therefore, the assumed surface supports half of the weight. Consequently, the force at point **B**, which is adjacent to it, is equivalent to the weight of **H-A-B-L**. The ratio of the distance to **D-A** is as the ratio of weight **H-A-B-L** to force **B**. The degree to which the load rises from the ground is the same degree to which less force is required. Thus, the load is placed in a position where it requires no force at all if the surface extended from mark **E**—which stands **<term>perpendicular [al-qa'im]</term>** <gloss>6</gloss> <note>The manuscript mistakenly reads "the pen" (*al-qalam*) instead of "perpendicular" (*al-qa'im*)</note> to the horizon—divides the load <gloss>4</gloss> <note>The manuscript reads "one of them" (*ahaduha*)</note> into two equal halves. 

This operation regarding the load is attributed to the properties of the circle, though it is not considered its primary application. However, for anyone who considers the **<term>bearing [al-mahmal]</term>** to also be attributed to the circle, the point is clear, because the circle is effectively a type of **<term>balance [mizan]</term>**.

One of them is small, which is the circle of the axle itself, and the other is large, which is the circle of the pulley-wheel. It is for this reason—and rightfully so—that the weight is suspended from the axle, while the moving force is applied to the pulley-wheel. This is because...

[8] Since this has been correctly established for us in our visualization of the circle, we now wish to demonstrate this regarding these **five powers** <note>The "five powers" are the five simple machines described by ancient engineers like Hero of Alexandria: the lever, the windlass, the pulley, the wedge, and the screw</note> and clarify their proof following this operation. The ancients who preceded us used to provide this introduction. Let us now clarify this using the instrument called the **<term>lever [al-mahal]</term>** <note>original: "al-mahal". This term refers to a mechanical lever or bar used to move heavy objects</note>.

This lever moves weights in two ways: either by being placed in a position parallel to the ground, or by being elevated and inclined away from the ground. Its operation involves pressing its elevated end down toward the ground. 

Let it first be parallel to the ground. Let the lever be line **AB**. Let the weight moved by the lever be at mark **A**, which is weight **C**. Let the **moving force** be at mark **B**. Let the stone beneath the lever, upon which the lever rotates, be at mark **D** <note>This "stone" serves as the fulcrum of the lever</note>. Let line **BD** be greater than line **DA**. 

If we lift the end of the lever at mark **B** and raise the lever above the stone it rotates upon, then weight **C** moves in the opposite direction. Mark **B** traces a circle around center **D**, and mark **A** also traces a circle around this same center, which is smaller than the circle traced by mark **B**. 

If the **<term>ratio [nisba]</term>** of line **BD** to **DA** is equal to the ratio of weight **C** to the force at **B**, then weight **C** balances force **B**. However, if the ratio of **BD** to **DA** is greater than the ratio of the weight to the force, then the force overcomes the weight. This is because they are two circles sharing a single center; the weight lies on an arc of the smaller circle, and the force...

<meta>This page is numbered 50 in the manuscript. It follows page 51, which introduced the lever (al-mahal). While the page numbering appears to be descending, the content continues the mathematical proof of how simple machines—specifically the lever and the wheel and axle—utilize ratios to move heavy objects with minimal effort.</meta>

->--- ( 50 ) ---<-

Having completed our mention and explanation of that, we shall now describe the cause by which each of these instruments pulls massive weights with a **<term>slight force [al-quwwa al-yasira]</term>**. 

We propose this as follows: let us assume two circles on a single center, which is mark **A**. Let their diameters be lines **BC** and **DE**. Let the two circles be movable around mark **A**, which is at their center<gloss>their centers</gloss>. Let the two circles be perpendicular to the horizon. Let us suspend two equal weights from marks **B** and **C**, namely weights **Z** and **H**. 

It will then appear to us that the circles do not tilt in any direction, because weights **Z** and **H** are equal and the distances **BA** and **AC** are equal. Thus, **BC** acts as a **<term>balance [mizan]</term>** <note>A simple beam scale where the pivot or fulcrum is in the center</note> rotating on a suspension point at mark **A**. 

If we then move the weight that was at **C** to mark **E**, it will tilt downwards, causing weight **Z** to descend and rotating the circles. If we then increase weight **T**, it will balance weight **Z**, and the **<term>ratio [nisba]</term>** of weight **T** to weight **Z** will be as the ratio of distance **BA** to distance **AE**. In this way, line **BE** can be envisioned as a balance rotating on a suspension point at mark **A**. 

**Archimedes** has already demonstrated this in his book **<term>On the Equilibrium of Planes</term>** <note>original: "Musawat al-Mayl," literally "The Equality of Inclination." This is Archimedes' foundational work on the law of the lever and centers of gravity</note>. 

It becomes evident from this that it is possible to move a great mass with a slight force. For if there are two circles on a single center, and a great weight is placed upon an arc of the large circle, and the ratio of the radius of the larger circle to the radius of the smaller circle is greater than the ratio of the great weight to the slight force moving it, then the slight force will overcome the great weight. ۝

...the end of the **<term>screw [lawlab]</term>** <note>original: "al-lawlab." The screw is the fifth of the five simple machines described by ancient engineers like Hero of Alexandria.</note> that protrudes from the fixed support. We turn the screw in this manner, and the load is raised. 

As for the **<term>screw-groove [hafr lawlabi]</term>** <note>This refers to the threading carved into the cylinder.</note> which is found on the cylinder, it may be **square** or it may be **lenticular** <note>original: "'adasi," literally "lentil-shaped," referring to a V-shaped or triangular thread.</note>. 

The **square** thread is the upright groove whose carving ends at two lines <note>This describes a flat-bottomed thread where the walls are perpendicular to the cylinder</note>. The **lenticular** thread is the one whose carving is slanted and ends at a single line; this is called lenticular, and the other is called square.

### [6] The Combined Use of the Screw

If the screw is used individually by itself, it is used in this manner. However, if it is used for another application by combining it with another power—the power that acts upon the **<term>axle [mihwar]</term>** upon which a **<term>composite disk [falka]</term>** <note>In this context, the 'falka' or disk acts like a gear wheel with pegs.</note> is mounted—it is as follows: 

We imagine the disk on the axle to have **pegs** <note>original: "awtad," serving as the teeth of a gear.</note>. We place a screw parallel to the disk, either standing upright on the ground or parallel to the surface of the ground. Let the pegs be fitted into the screw-groove. The ends of the screw are held in two round holes within two fixed supports, as we described previously. 

Let the end of the screw have an extension protruding from the fixed support. In this extension, we fit a **square handle-base** with grips, or we drill holes in that extension so we can fit pegs into them to turn the screw. 

When we wish to lift a weight with this machine, we tighten the **<term>cable [al-muqallas]</term>** <note>A thick, heavy-duty rope used for hoisting.</note> that is attached to the load-carrier onto the axle on both sides of the disk. We then turn the screw in which we have engaged the pegs of the disk. As we turn, the disk and the axle rotate together, and the weight is lifted <note>This describes a worm gear mechanism, where the rotation of the screw (the "worm") drives the pegged wheel (the "gear") to provide immense mechanical advantage.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the geometric and mechanical description of the screw (lawlab). While the previous page focused on the practical assembly of a screw-jack with a bolt and guide-rail, this page steps back to provide a formal geometric definition of how a spiral is generated and how a screw thread is plotted onto a cylinder by using a right-angled triangle.</meta>

—•• ( 47 ) ••—

The line drawn upon it is as follows: if we imagine one of the edges <note>The text uses "side" (dil‘) to refer to the height or the "generator" of the cylinder.</note> of a moving **cylindrical shape** on the surface of the cylinder, and we imagine a point at the end of that edge moving along it and traversing its entire length in the same amount of time it takes for that edge to rotate once around the entire surface of the cylindrical shape and return to its starting position, then the line that the point traces on the surface of the cylindrical shape will be a **<term>spiral line [da’ira lawlabiyya]</term>**, which is what we call the **screw**.

If we wish to draw this line on the surface of the cylinder, we use the following procedure: we assume a surface where there are two lines, one of them standing perpendicular to the other at a **right angle**. One of these lines is equal to the side of the cylinder, and the other is equal to the circumference of the cylinder—that is, the circle of its base. We then connect the ends of the two lines at the right angle with a line that **subtends the right angle** <note>This is the hypotenuse of a right-angled triangle. Geometrically, if you wrap a right triangle around a cylinder, the hypotenuse becomes the spiral thread.</note>.

Next, we align the line equal to the cylinder’s side with the side of the cylinder, and the line equal to the circumference of the cylinder’s base with the base of the cylinder. Consequently, the line subtending the right angle will wrap around the surface of the cylinder, forming a **spiral line** upon it. 

We can also divide the side of the cylinder into as many equal parts as we wish and draw a spiral line on each part. In this way, there will be many spiral lines on the cylinder, and the cylinder itself becomes a **screw**. A cylinder around which the hypotenuse of the angle has been wrapped is called a **single-turn screw**—that is, when the side of the cylinder encloses only a single line...

### [4] The Fourth Power
As for the fourth power that follows this, it is the power called the **Wedge** <term>al-asfīn</term>. It is used in certain instruments for perfumes <note>original: "al-tib"—likely referring to presses for aromatic oils or medicinal preparations</note>, and for joining what has come apart in carpentry work. However, its most frequent use is when we wish to separate the base of a stone that we intend to cut, having already separated its sides from the mountain from which we are quarrying it. In this particular matter, none of the other powers are effective, even if they were all combined; only the **wedge** alone can accomplish this. Its action is through the **blow** <note>The impact of a hammer or mallet.</note> it receives, whatever that blow may be. Its effect does not cease after the blow has settled; it is evident to us that even without many strikes, it often produces a sound and uproots what it splits by its force. Whenever the angle of the wedge is smaller, the work performed with it is easier, as we shall demonstrate. ❧

The fifth power is the one called the **Screw** <term>al-lawlab</term> <gloss>3</gloss>. However, regarding the **screw**, there is difficulty in its construction and use, whether it operates alone or another power operates alongside it. It is, in fact, nothing other than a **twisted wedge** <note>This is a classic mechanical definition: a screw thread is essentially an inclined plane or wedge wrapped around a cylinder.</note> which is not struck, but rather moves by a load. This will become clear from what we are about to mention. We say that the nature...

<meta>This page (35) discusses the third "power," the pulley system, providing the technical mechanical details that precede the discussion of wedges and screws found on page 46. It explains how adding pulleys to both fixed beams and moving loads reduces the effort required for lifting.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>35</page-num>

If we pull the **cable** <term>al-qals</term> <note>A heavy-duty rope used in mechanical lifting.</note>, our movement of that weight becomes easier. If we tie another **pulley** <term>bakara</term> to the **fixed crossbeam** <term>al-ʿāriḍa al-thābita</term>, pass the end of the cable through it, and pull it, our movement of that weight will be even more effortless. 

Furthermore, if we fasten another pulley to the weight itself and pass the end of the rope through it, that increases our ease in moving the weight. Following this procedure, we may increase the number of pulleys on both the fixed crossbeam and on the weight we wish to carry. We then pass one of the ends of the cable through the fixed pulley and through the one attached to the **load** <term>al-maḥmal</term>. Wherever the cable runs and is pulled toward it <note>The manuscript reading is "it extends."</note>, we increase the ease of lifting that weight. The more pulleys over which the cable runs, the easier it becomes to lift that weight. 

It is necessary for one end of the cable to be fixed and tied to the fixed crossbeam, with the cable running from there to the weight. As for the pulleys on the fixed crossbeam, they should be fastened to another piece of wood and rotate on a single **axle** <term>miḥwar</term> <note>The manuscript uses the feminine form of "one."</note>. This axle is called a **manganon** <note>original: μαγγανον — a Greek term for a mechanical device or pulley-block</note>. That piece of wood is then fastened to the fixed crossbeam by another cable. 

As for the pulleys fastened to the load, they are placed on another axle equal to that axle <note>The manuscript repeats "that."</note> and tied to the load. These must be mounted on the axle in such a way that it is impossible for some to rotate without the others; for if they were difficult to rotate, we said—

<meta>This page (22) describes the mechanical advantage of the lever before transitioning to the "Third Power," the pulley system. Although the page number in the OCR (22) precedes the previous page provided (35), the content focuses on the evolution of simple machines, moving from the lever (the "first power") to the pulley. The text explains the transition from direct lifting to mechanical assistance.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>22</page-num>

Perhaps this power was the first to be considered for the movement of excessively heavy bodies. This is because when people wanted to move an extremely heavy object, the first thing they needed to do was lift it off the ground. However, they had no handles to grasp it by, as all of its parts were resting flat on the earth. Consequently, they had to devise a clever solution: they dug a shallow hole in the ground beneath the heavy body, took a long beam of wood, and inserted its tip into that hole. When they pressed down on the other end of the beam, the weight was lifted. 

They then placed a stone under this beam, which they called the **hypomochlion** <term>abumikhliyun</term> <note>original: ὑπομόχλιον — a Greek term meaning "that which is under the lever," or what we now call a fulcrum.</note>. This name signifies "that which is placed under the load." When they pressed down again, the weight rose even further. Once this power became apparent, it was understood that great weights could be moved using this method. This beam is called a **lever** <term>mikhlan</term>, whether it is round or square in shape. The closer the stone placed beneath it is to the weight being moved, the easier the movement becomes, as we shall explain in detail further on.

### The Third Power

As for the third power, it is the one called the **lifting gear** <term>al-kabīra al-rafʿ</term>. When we wish to lift any weight, we tie **cables** <term>al-qulūs</term> <note>Large, heavy-duty ropes used for mechanical work.</note> to that weight. If we were to pull the cables directly to lift it, we would require a force equal to the weight we wish to raise. However, if we detach the cable from the load and tie one of its ends to a **fixed crossbeam** <term>ʿāriḍa thābita</term>, then pass the other end through a **pulley** <term>bakara</term> <note>A wheel with a grooved rim for a rope, used to change the direction of force.</note> that is fastened to the middle of the load...

...and the axle together. This wheel is called the **peritrochion** <term>barbiṭrakhun</term> <note>original: περιτρόχιον — The manuscript shows this word was at one point erased or struck through. It refers to the "wheel and axle" mechanism, literally meaning "that which turns around."</note>, and its meaning is "the encircling one." When we do this, we should make a flat indentation in the axle on both sides of the wheel, so that this indentation acts as a spool upon which the **cables** <term>al-qulūs</term> <note>Heavy-duty ropes used for hauling.</note> can wrap. 

We then bore holes into the outer surface of the wheel—that is, along its circumference—as many as are needed. These should be precisely made <note>The original Arabic term *muhandamah* suggests they are well-engineered or planed to fit perfectly.</note> so that when **pegs** <term>awtād</term> are fitted into them, the wheel and the axle rotate together by means of these pegs. We have already explained how the axle should be constructed; now we shall explain how to operate it.

If you wish to move a heavy weight with a force less than the weight itself, tie the cables attached to the weight to the designated area on the axle on both sides of the wheel. Then, insert the pegs into the holes we bored in the wheel and press the pegs downwards to rotate the wheel. Consequently, the weight will move with only a small amount of force, and the cables will wind around the axle, perhaps even overlapping one another so they do not spread too far across the length of this axle. The size of this machine must be proportional to the size of the heavy objects you wish to move. As for its specific proportions, they must be based on the ratio between the weight we wish to move and the power applied to move it; we shall demonstrate this in the upcoming sections.

### [2] The Second Power

As for the second power, it is the one called the **lever** <term>al-mukhal</term> <note>The manuscript here uses the term *al-maḥal*, which is likely a scribal variant or error for *al-mukhul*, the lever.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the construction and operation of the "wheel and axle" (peritrochion), explaining how pegs and cables allow a small force to move a large weight. It concludes by introducing the lever as the "Second Power" of mechanics.</summary>
<keywords>wheel and axle, peritrochion, mechanical advantage, cables, axle, pegs, Hero of Alexandria, five powers of mechanics</keywords>

->In the name of God, the Merciful, the Compassionate<-

## The Second Discourse from the Book of Hero on Lifting Heavy Weights

[1] Since the powers by which a known weight is moved by a known force are five, it is necessary that we set forth their forms, their uses, and their names. For though these powers are all related to a single physical principle, they differ greatly in their forms. Their names are as follows: the **axle** within a **wheel**, the **lever**, the **pulley**, the **wedge**, and the **screw**.

Regarding the **axle** <term>miḥwar</term> fitted into a **wheel** <term>falkah</term> <note>While *falkah* can mean a celestial sphere or a spindle-whorl, here it refers specifically to the drum or wheel component of the windlass.</note>, it is constructed in this manner: A solid, square piece of wood is taken in the form of a beam; its ends are smoothed and rounded. Upon these ends, precisely engineered bronze **rings** <insert>1</insert> <note>original: *sarinjāt* — likely derived from the Persian *asrabjah*, referring to metal bushings or collars used to reduce friction.</note> are mounted. The thickness of the axle must be carefully sized so that when it is fitted into a circular hole—which we also line with bronze—within a fixed, immobile support, it rotates easily. When a wooden beam is crafted in this specific way, it is called an **axle** <term>miḥwar</term>. 

Then, a wheel with a square hole is mounted in the middle of the axle. This hole must be the same size as the middle of the axle and precisely fitted to it, so that when the wheel is mounted on the axle, the wheel and axle rotate together as one.

<summary>Hero of Alexandria introduces the five simple machines of the ancient world and provides a technical guide for constructing a low-friction axle and wheel assembly using bronze bushings.</summary>

<keywords>Hero of Alexandria, simple machines, five powers, mechanics, axle, wheel, pulley, lever, wedge, screw, bronze bushings, engineering</keywords>

While the previous page began describing the specific components of the axle and wheel belonging to Book Two, this text acts as a bridge, summarizing the introductory section and looking forward to the detailed study of the "five powers" of mechanics.</meta>

This concludes this section. Regarding the first of the two discourses on the introductions to the **art of mechanics** <term>ṣināʿat al-ḥiyal</term> <note>original: "ṣināʿat al-ḥiyal"—Literally translated as "the art of ingenious devices," this was the standard Arabic term for the study of mechanics and engineering.</note>, this suffices. 

As for that which follows, we shall provide an account of the **five powers** by which **weights** <term>athqāl</term> are moved and drawn, as well as the natural principles governing them. We shall also discuss other matters that are of great utility in the carrying and lifting of weights. 

Thus ends the first discourse of the Book of **Hero** <note>Hero of Alexandria, a Greek mathematician and engineer active in the 1st century AD. His works were preserved and expanded upon by medieval Arabic scholars.</note> on Lifting Heavy Objects.

### 30

[34] Let there be a **disk** <term>falkah</term> or a **pulley** <term>bakrah</term> moving on an **axle** <term>miḥwar</term> at a **center** <term>markaz</term> **A** <note>The Arabic letter 'Alif' (أ) is used here for the point A.</note>. Let its **diameter** <term>quṭr</term> be the line **BC**, and let it be **parallel to the horizon**. We shall suspend two ropes from points **B** and **C**, namely **ZH** and **HT** <note>The letters correspond to specific points on the diagram in the original manuscript. The translator has used the standard equivalents for the Arabic letters: B (ب), C (ج), Z (ز), H (هـ), and T (ط).</note>, and we shall hang equal **weights** <term>athqāl</term> from them.

It will be apparent to us that the pulley does not incline toward any direction because the two weights are equal and the distances from the point of suspension **A** are equal.

Now, let the weight at **C** be greater than the weight at **E**. It will then be apparent to us that the disk inclines toward the side of **B** <note>original: "ميل الى جهة ب" — The text likely contains a scribal error here, as a heavier weight at C should cause an inclination toward C. A marginal note in the manuscript also suggests a reversal of letters.</note>, and point **B** descends along with the weight. We must then determine at which position the greater weight **C** will come to rest when it descends.

Let us lower point **B** and bring it to point **Z**, and let rope **HT** be placed upon rope **ZH**; thus the weight comes to rest. It appears to us that rope **HT** wraps around the rim of the disk and is suspended with the weight at point **C**, because the portion of the rope that is wrapped is no longer freely suspended. Thus, **ZH** extends to point **C**. Because the two weights are in equilibrium, the **ratio** <term>nisbah</term> of the weight is as the ratio of the distance between the suspension point **A** and the ropes. 

Thus, as the distance **AC** is to **AE**, so is the weight at **C** to the weight at **E**. If we set the ratio of **CA** to **AE** to be equal to the ratio of the weight to the weight, and we extend lines from points **B** and **C** toward **D** and **T** at **right angles** <term>zawāyā qāʾimah</term>, it appears to us that the disk has moved from point **B** to point **Z** and comes to rest.

This statement also applies to other weights; therefore, it is possible for every weight to be balanced by a weight smaller than it on—

It appears to us that the weight only moves when the force of the second man is added. However, it is clear that the second man alone cannot move the weight; for if the first man slacks off and leaves it to the second, he will not move it. But if the weight were divided into two halves, the first man alone could move one half, while the other half remained stationary. Thus, it appears to us that the half which the single man moved was of the same **magnitude** <note>original: "jasada," referring to the physical bulk or bodily substance of the object.</note> as the other half before it was separated from it. Now, let us place a support under mark **G**, which is support **GD**. It then becomes clear also that support **AB** receives half the weight of **EA**, support **DG** receives half the weight of **EG**, and support **EZ** receives half of the weight of **AG**.

Before we placed support **GD**, we demonstrated how much of the weight each of **AB** and **EZ** receives. It is now evident to us that when support **GD** was placed under the weight, support **AB** began to receive more of the weight than it did previously, by an amount of half of **EH**—that is, by half of **EG**. Consequently, the weight received by **EZ** became less than what it received initially by the amount of **EG**. 

Therefore, that which **DG** receives of the weight, according to this reasoning, is half of **EG**. This is because the support that was added under the weight decreased what **EZ** receives by an amount equal to the weight of **EG**, and increased the load on support **AB** by a weight equal to half of **EG**. Thus, **GD** receives half the weight of **EG**, and the remainder follows. This magnitude was already being received according to the other **distribution** <note>original: "al-ʿadl al-ākhar," referring to a balanced state or a previous method of calculation.</note>. # — ( 33 ) —

Let there be upon it a certain **body** <term>jism</term> of equal size and **weight** <term>al-thuql</term> <note>In this context, weight refers to the downward gravitational pressure exerted by a uniform mass.</note>, and let it be designated **AG**. We have already stated that each of the two **supports** <term>qawā’im</term> <note>These are the vertical pillars or points of contact holding the object up.</note> **AB** and **GD** receives half of the weight of **AG**.

Now, let us move support **GD** and bring it closer to **AB**, and let its new position be **EZ**. We wish to know as well what portion of the weight **AB** and **EZ** receive. We say that the distance **AH** must either be equal to the distance **HG**, or smaller than it, or greater than it.

Suppose it is equal to it; it then appears to us that the weight of **AH** balances the weight of **HG**. If we were to remove support **AB**, **AG** would remain stable in its current state. From this, it appears to us that support **AB** received none of the weight, but rather the entire weight of **AG** rested upon **EZ** alone. 

However, if the distance **GH** is greater than the distance **HA**, then the weight of **AG** inclines toward the side of **G**. Now, let the distance **GH** be smaller than the distance **HA**, and let **GH** be equal to **AH**. Thus, **GH** would be balanced upon **EZ** alone. Let us place their joint at **HT**. If we imagine that the entire weight is concentrated at mark **H**, then **HG** would remain stable upon **EZ** alone, and half of **AH** would be supported by each of the two supports **AB** and **HT**.

If we then remove support **HT**, mark **H** possesses the force of the support once the body is joined. Consequently, **AB** receives half the weight of **HA**, and **EZ** receives the remainder—that is, **GH** and half of **AH**. This means that if we imagine **AG** divided into two parts at mark **K**, where **KE** <note>The text reads 'KH' or 'KE' depending on the scribal stroke; based on the geometry of halves, it refers to the segment midpoint.</note> is half of **AH**, then if the support that was initially at **E** is placed under mark **K**, it receives the weight of the entire **AG**. Whenever the support moves further from the division that splits the weight into two halves...

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof for the distribution of weight on a beam when supports are shifted. It demonstrates that as a support moves toward the center of gravity, it carries more of the load, eventually supporting the entire weight of the object.</summary>

<meta>This page is numbered 32 in the transcription, following the logic of the previous page (numbered 33 in its translation) which introduced the concept of weight distribution across supports. This page moves from a theoretical two-support system to a more complex arrangement involving three or more cylinders, establishing the mathematical ratios for how weight is shared when multiple supports are placed under a continuous or divided body.</meta>

Regarding the **weight** <term>al-thuql</term>, let it be imagined that weight **AB** is divided at mark **E** along a line perpendicular to a **cylinder** <term>ustuwānah</term> <note>The author uses "cylinder" to refer to the vertical columns or pillars acting as supports.</note>. It appears to us that from the side of **AE**, each of the two cylinders **AG** and **EZ** receives half of its weight. On the side of **EB**, each of the two cylinders **EZ** and **BD** receives half of its weight. This is because there is no difference in what the **columns** <term>asāṭīn</term> receive whether the object placed upon them is continuous or separate; since it is all upon the cylinders, whether joined or divided.

Thus, cylinder **EZ** receives half the weight of **EB** and half the weight of **AE**—meaning half of the entire weight of **AB**. Cylinder **AG** receives half the weight of **AE**, and cylinder **BD** receives half the weight of **EB**. If we divide half of **AB** according to the ratio of the distance **AE** <gloss>The manuscript reads: "the position".</gloss> to the distance **EB**, then the weight of the part corresponding to the ratio of **AE** is assigned to **AG**, and the weight corresponding to the distance **EB** is received by **BD**.

Furthermore, let us place another cylinder, which is **HT**. It then appears to us that **AG** receives half of **AE**, and **BD** receives half of **EB**. Cylinder **EZ** receives half of **AH**, and cylinder **HT** receives half of **EB**, half of **AE**, half of **HB**, and half of **AH** <gloss>The manuscript reads: "AB".</gloss> <gloss>The manuscript omits these words: "and half of AH".</gloss>. This sum of halves is the whole of **AB**, which is the total weight placed upon all the cylinders. If there are more cylinders, we can know by this calculation how much weight each one of them receives.

Regarding the aspect of quantity that is suitable for an introduction: Archimedes has utilized a masterful technique in this section of his book known as the **Book of Supports** <note>original: *Kitāb al-Qawā’im*. This likely refers to a lost or fragmented mechanical work by Archimedes regarding the distribution of weights on pillars, known in Latin as *De columnis*.</note>. We shall set forth what is needed from it for other applications. For now, let us apply those principles of quantity in a manner suitable for students.

The method is as follows: if there are a certain number of **columns** <term>asāṭīn</term> supporting beams or a wall, and the placement of these supports is either equal or varies at the ends—such that the load extends beyond one end or both ends—and whether the distance between the columns is equal or different; we wish to know how much of the **weight** <term>al-thuql</term> each individual column bears.

The same applies if there is a long piece of wood of uniform <gloss>The manuscript reads: "joint" or "collective".</gloss> weight carried by men positioned equally along the length of the wood and at its ends, where one or all of the ends might protrude. We want to know how much of the weight each man receives, for the requirement in all these cases is the same. ❦

[36] Let there be a weight of uniform thickness and equal parts resting upon the columns, designated as <note>Letters with overbars denote geometric segments</note> **AB**. Let it be placed upon two cylinders, which are **AG** and **DB**. In this case, each of the two cylinders, **AG** and **DB**, receives half of the weight of **AB**. 

Now, let there be another cylinder, which is **EZ**. Let the distance **AB** be partitioned in whatever way it falls. We wish to know how much each of the columns **AG**, **EZ**, and **BD** receives.

--- Page 295 ---
<meta>This page is numbered 30 in the transcription, following page 31 from the previous section. The text continues the geometric derivation of the center of gravity, shifting from the specific example of columns to a more abstract proof involving intersecting planes and lines.</meta>

Page 30

The **equilibrium point** <term>‘alāmat al-i‘tidāl</term> <note>This refers to the point where a body balances perfectly.</note> indicates to us another point as well, different from the first. In this case, a situation arises similar to what occurred in the first instance: namely, that the line projected through that point passes through the center of the body. If the two lines are distant from one another and two planes are projected upon them, they might not intersect; indeed, it is possible for two planes to be projected on two lines without intersecting. If this were to occur here, it would lead to the same problem as before, which is impossible.

Because of this, it is understood that the planes must intersect and the lines must meet, thereby existing in a single plane. When that plane is extended to the **surface of the body** <term>basīṭ al-jism</term>, it forms a line at the **point of intersection** <term>‘alāmat al-taqāṭu‘</term>. A third point exists externally upon this line. By conceptualizing this balancing point, the body is likewise held in equilibrium upon it. One may then project a line from this point onto the surface of the body.

According to our previous discussion, if this line is projected, it falls exactly upon the line where the two planes were projected; it does not fall on any point other than their meeting point. This is because if a line meets two intersecting lines while situated in another plane, it must meet them specifically at their point of intersection. If it did not meet them at their point of intersection, then part of the line would have to exist in one plane and the remainder in another plane. Therefore, all lines related to this point must converge at a single point, which is called the **center of inclination and gravity** <term>markaz al-mayl wa-al-thuql</term>. ❦ <note>The Arabic letter 'H' (ﻫ) here functions as a punctuation mark to signify the end of the proof.</note>

It is now necessary, by logical requirement, that we clarify certain matters regarding **compression** <term>al-kabs</term>, **transferring** <term>al-naql</term>, and **carrying** <term>al-ḥaml</term>. <note>These terms likely refer to the mechanical forces involved in pressing weights, moving them, and the structural load-bearing capacity of supports.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a geometric proof showing that all lines of balance in a solid body must converge at a single point, defined here as the center of gravity. It then transitions into a new discussion on the mechanical principles of compression and transport.</summary>

The points in which the **weight** <term>al-thiql</term> is suspended are those that carry the weight. As for the **center of inclination** <term>markaz al-mayl</term> <note>The historical term for the center of gravity.</note>, it is a single unique mark in every body to which the **lines of suspension** <term>al-‘alāqāt</term>¹ are attributed. 

The centers of inclination in some bodies may lie outside of their **physical substance** <term>jawāhirihā</term>, as occurs in **rings**² and bracelets <note>In a hollow ring, the center of gravity is in the empty space in the middle.</note>. As for the fact that the lines of suspension meet at a single point common to them, this becomes clear to us if we imagine a certain **plane** <term>saṭḥ</term> standing perpendicularly to the horizon. Every plane that cuts a body with uniformity will show us that the body is divided into two [equal] halves. 

For when this plane penetrates the body, and we imagine another plane cutting the body in the same manner, it also penetrates it just as the first plane did, and the two **planes**³ intersect upon a line. If the intersection occurred on a line other than the line of suspension, it would result in the bodies being balanced and unbalanced at the same time <note>A logical contradiction used to prove that the center of gravity must lie on this intersection.</note>. 

Let us now transfer this discourse to the **lines of suspension**⁴ and imagine a body standing upon a **line**⁵. Let the body be of uniform parts, standing upon that line. When that line is projected, it penetrates the body. If the projected line were to fall outside the body, then the plane projected upon it would also fall outside the body, and that has already been shown to be impossible. Therefore, the line must penetrate the body and divide it into two equal parts. So let...

### [32]
If we wish to carry a **weight** <term>thiql</term> toward a higher direction, we then require a **force** <term>quwwa</term> equal to that weight. Let us imagine a high, movable **pulley** <term>ḥanāya</term> <note>The term *ḥanāya* literally means "arches" or "curves," but the context and the medieval editor's note confirm it refers to a pulley wheel here.</note> standing upon a surface, moving upon centers around an **axis** <term>miḥwar</term> with an easy motion. I say that this weight moves by a force equal to it. There is not a "force" at the other end of the rope in the abstract, but rather another **weight** <note>original: "thiql ākhar"</note> tied to it. It appears to us that if the weights are equal, the pulley does not incline toward any direction. The first weight cannot overcome the second tied weight, nor can the weight overcome the load, because the second tied weight is equal to the first load. 

However, if the weight is increased by just enough to cause motion, then the other weight is pulled toward the higher direction. Therefore, the force that moves the load—if it is greater than the load—overcomes it and moves it, unless there occurs some roughness in the rotation of the pulley or stiffness in the **sheaves** <term>fulūs</term> <note>original: "fulūs"; likely referring to the small internal discs or pins within the pulley mechanism that might cause friction.</note>, which would result in the prevention of movement. <image-desc>floral terminal mark</image-desc>

### [21]
The waters that are on surfaces which are not inclined are themselves not inclined; rather, they remain stationary and do not lean toward any direction. However, if the surface is tilted by even the slightest inclination, the water gathers and flows toward that direction until not even the smallest part of the water remains stable upon it—unless there are hollows in the surface. In that case, small amounts of water remain in the bottom of those hollows, just as happens in vessels. 

This occurs with water because its parts are not joined together with extreme hardness <note>The author is describing fluid dynamics versus solid mechanics; water lacks the internal cohesion (viscosity/rigidity) of solids.</note>. As for **solid bodies** <term>al-ajsād al-muttaṣila</term>, because they are by nature not perfectly smooth across their surfaces, it is not easy for them to move. It happens that because of the **roughness of bodies** <term>khashūnat al-ajsād</term> <note>This is an early description of friction.</note>, they support one another. The result is that they lean against each other like **teeth** <note>original: *al-aḍrās*; likely referring to the way rough surfaces interlock like gear teeth or molars.</note>. 

This was designed so <note>Referring to the mechanical solution for friction.</note> because when these bodies are numerous and joined together by gathering one to another, they require a massive amount of **force** <term>quwwa</term> to be moved. Thus, people developed techniques: they began to lay small wooden beams <term>khushaybāt</term> beneath the **heavy frames** <term>thikhānāt</term> <note>original: *al-fakhānāt*; the editor suggests this may refer to a 'tortoise' (*al-iḥāyāt*), a heavy structure or siege engine used to protect workers.</note>. These wooden beams are shaped like **columns** <term>asāṭīn</term> <note>In this context, cylinders or rollers.</note> so that they only touch a very small part of the surface. From this, only the slightest amount of roughness <note>friction</note> occurs. 

They also use **wedges** <term>awtād</term>, and the weight moves upon them easily, even though the weight of the apparatus and the structure is added to the load itself. They lay carved planks upon the surface, chosen for their smoothness, and coat them with a **soft substance** <note>A lubricant, such as grease, oil, or soap.</note> which reduces the roughness upon them. Thus, they move the weight with the slightest force. As for the columns <note>rollers</note>, when they...

# ( 23 )

...equivalent to it, through their use of **false opinions** <note>original: *al-ārāʾ al-kādhiba*; the author is likely refuting common misconceptions in ancient mechanics, possibly the Aristotelian idea that a constant great force is required to maintain any motion.</note>. Let us then demonstrate that the **weights** <term>al-athqāl</term> placed according to our description move with a **force** <term>quwwa</term> less than any known force, and we shall clarify the reason why this is not apparent in practical work.

Let us imagine a certain **mass** <term>jumlan mā</term> placed upon the ground; let it be level and smooth, with its parts joined together. Let the **surface** <term>al-saṭḥ</term> upon which the weight rests be capable of tilting in both directions—I mean, to the right and to the left. 

First, let it be tilted to the right. It will appear to us that the given weight inclines toward the right side, because it is the nature of weights to move **downward** <term>al-sifl</term> unless something prevents them from **moving** <term>al-ḥaraka</term>. 

Likewise, if the surface is righted and becomes **level** <term>muʿtadila</term>, the weight is supported by this. If it then tilts to the other side—that is, to the left—the weight also descends toward that tilted side, even if the **inclination** <term>al-mayl</term> is very slight. This is because the weight would require a force to push against it to prevent it from moving <note>The author is describing the "limit" of equilibrium; on a perfect slope, any gravity component causes motion.</note>. 

If the weight then becomes level and is not tilted toward any of the directions, it remains at rest without needing a force to hold it back. It will remain tranquil until the surface tilts in whichever direction it may; then it inclines toward that direction, for the weight is prepared to go in any direction. Thus, its requirement to move is only a slight force, equal to the force that tilts it. Therefore, the weight moves by any slight force.

to the other perpendicular. I mean: let the common line for the two tablets be line AB. 
Let the ends of the side of the triangle be markers C and D, and the assigned marker 
be marker E. Let us extend a perpendicular to line CD, which is perpendicular Z. Let us extend line 
DH equal to line CZ, but let line HT be equal to line EZ, which stands 
upon it. The end of the rod is not directed toward marker E, but 
rather toward marker T. In this way, we manage its transfer to the other side, 
and thus we create the limbs of the bodies in a mirrored fashion.

[20] And some people have thought that the weights <term>al-athqāl</term> placed on the ground are moved by a force... <note>original: *bi-quwwa*. This marks the beginning of the theoretical proof found on the following page regarding why any weight can be moved by any force on a frictionless surface.</note>

<sig>3.</sig>

Likewise, we also extend the rod <term>al-qaḍīb</term> <note>A measuring bar or gauge used to transfer dimensions from a model to the material being worked.</note> over the protruding parts <gloss>1</gloss> <note>Manuscript 1 reads: "the year" [al-sunnah], likely a scribal error for "the mark" or "the point" [al-simah or al-sinnah].</note> and we draw the similarly positioned features on the stone. Then, we work the plane <term>al-basīṭ</term> <note>The flat surface or "face" of the stone block.</note> according to the designated markers. These are the markers by which the shape’s proportions were transferred to correspond to the shape previously placed, becoming related to it in the mentioned ratio. 

As for the parallel line we mentioned, it is drawn on the other tablet <term>al-lawḥ</term> with ease if we draw a certain line on the tablet that is parallel to the common line. That the shapes produced by this process are similar is evident, because they are formed from protruding shapes in similar positions; their bases are the triangles drawn by the ends of the two rods in each of the bodies. 

That there is a known ratio between them is also evident, because the parallel shapes created behind the bodies have a ratio that is three <gloss>3</gloss> <note>Manuscript 3 reads: "for their ratios, three." This refers to the geometric principle where the volume of similar solids is proportional to the cube (the "triple ratio") of their corresponding sides.</note> times the proportional sides, for the sides of the similar triangles were so assumed. Therefore, the solid bodies <term>al-mujassamāt</term> have this known ratio to one another <gloss>2</gloss> <note>Manuscript 2 reads: "from shapes."</note> <insert>[14]</insert>. 

If we wish to create what is behind the similar bodies, we employ this device on the rear side using three markers in each of the shapes, placed in a corresponding manner. This is achieved through the lines that create two triangles equal to the triangles made from these alignments.

Let us suppose that we wish to draw an eye in the likeness <term>mithāl</term> <note>Meaning a model, template, or statue.</note> of a human, or any other likeness. We place the centers of these instruments upon the model—that is, the object before us for which we wish to create a similar shape. We then bend the end of the lead rod <term>al-qaḍīb al-raṣāṣ</term> <note>A flexible measuring bar made of lead that can be shaped to match a contour and hold its form.</note> that is attached to them until its tip touches the eye we wish to replicate.

Then, we lift these and mount them upon the triangle <term>al-muthallath</term> that has been drawn on the tablets <term>al-lawḥ</term>. Next, we lower or raise the other tablet—the one that does not yet have a drawing on it—until the tip of the rod reaches it by its lowering or raising. We then lift these and connect two lines from the mark reached by the lead rod on the tablet to the ends of the triangle's side, which sits on the line shared by the two tablets.

We must keep each of the two tablets fixed and immovable relative to the other. From the other mark on the line shared by the tablets, we extend a line parallel <term>al-khaṭṭ al-muwāzī</term> to the major lines near the line parallel to the base <term>al-qāʿidah</term> until it intersects the other extended line. 

Then we take the other instrument and mount the ends of the points <term>al-asnān</term> <note>Literally "teeth," referring to the sharp tips or pins of the measuring tool.</note>—which were bent for the three points—upon the triangle in the tablet that is equal to the known triangle from the ends of those parts. We bend the lead rod until it reaches the mark drawn for the parallel line on the other tablet. 

Finally, we lift these and place them on the designated marks in the figure <term>shakl</term> we have not yet used. On whichever mark the tip of the rod overlaps on the physical body, that mark shall be the one positioned at the site of the eye for the similar likeness—the same position for which the first rod was originally bent.

<insert>[18]</insert> As for **solid shapes**, we construct them in the following manner: we take two **wooden tablets** with movable surfaces joined at a **common line** <note>This describes a hinge or a folding joint</note>. This common line remains a single, fixed axis throughout every movement. This is achieved if the centers of the **pivots** <term>Dwadāḥāt</term> <note>original: "dwadāḥāt" (دوادحات). A rare technical term. The scholarly footnote suggests it refers to "tourillons" or the pins/trunnions upon which the boards rotate.</note> on which the tablets move are positioned exactly on this common line.

The size of the tablets should correspond to the size of the larger of the two **similar solid shapes** being worked upon. This is the method of construction, and we make it according to the specific need. 

We must also take two **iron shapes** that resemble the letter called **Hula** <note>original: "Hūlā" (هولآ). As noted previously, this is a template or angular guide-tool.</note>. Let the measured segments of each of these be equal, and let their ends be bent with a **sharp angle**. From the bending of two of these, a **triangular shape** should be formed. 

Let the **known ratio** of one body to the other be **three times the ratio** <note>In medieval geometry, the "triple ratio" of 1D lines refers to the ratio of 3D volumes. If the lengths are in ratio $a:b$, the volumes are in the "triple ratio" $a^3:b^3$.</note> of the corresponding sides of the triangle to one another. 

Let this be imagined as lines **A-B, A-J, A-D**, and for the lines that have been bent, **H-E, B-Z, D-H**. For the other shape, let the lines be **T-K, T-L, T-M**, and for the lines that have been bent, **K-N, L-S, M-A**. 

Let the **similar triangles** be **H-E-Z** and **N-S-A**. We then draw on the common line of the two movable tablets—specifically on one of the tablets—a shape equal and similar to the iron shape. From one of the lines of the triangle, we extend a line **parallel to the base** of the triangle, so that it encompasses another triangle equal to the iron triangle.

<meta>This page serves as the geometric foundation for the instrument described on page 18. While the subsequent page focuses on the physical construction of wooden tablets and iron templates for 3D scaling, this page explains the mathematical theory of point-mapping. It describes how to use concentric divisions and radial lines to replicate a two-dimensional figure (circles) before expanding that logic to three-dimensional bodies (spheres).</meta>

<page-num>۱۷</page-num>

We place within it what follows the mark **H**. Let us assume a certain mark inside the figure **A-B-G-D-E-Z**, which is the mark **T**. Let us draw upon the two marks **H** and **T** two circles of equal surface area, and let us divide them into an equal number of parts at the marks **K-L-M-N-S-ʿA-F-S-Q-R-Sh-T-Th**. 

We then connect these points and extend them from the centers to the sections. These will produce lines equal to the lines in figure **A-B-G-D-E-Z** starting from mark **H**. 

Let line **A-K** be equal to line **Q-A**, and **L-B** to line **R-D**, and **M-G** to line **Sh-W**, and **W-D** to line **T-S**, and **W-E** to line **Th-Z**, and **W-Z** to line **F-Kh**. <note>These letter combinations represent specific coordinates used to map points from the original shape to the copy.</note>

Let us then extend lines through the marks **Kh, Dh, D, S, Z**, and similar marks. If the equal circles centered on **T** and **H** are divided into even more parts, the resulting drawing will be more accurate and precise, as the marks will be closer together. 

Thus, let us draw the line **Kh-Dh-D-S-Z**. This line will be equal and similar to the line **A-B-G-D-E-Z**, because the surfaces are similar and equal, coinciding with one another.

<insert>[17]</insert> In **solid shapes** <term>Shakl mujassam</term> <note>Three-dimensional geometric bodies, as opposed to flat "plane" figures.</note> as well, whether they are regular or irregular, one should imagine a similar transfer—meaning that a **sphere** <note>original: "kura" (كرة)</note> is used instead of a circle, or the shape is contained within it, or another equal and similar shape is used. 

We then assume marks upon it that are similar in their position, and we extend lines from them to other marks placed in the center of the shapes. When we do this, these lines will form a **solid shape** that is equal and similar to the shape originally positioned.

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>The text on this page (numbered 13 in the header) precedes the mechanical description found on the previously translated page 15. While the previous page focused on the physical assembly of a pantograph-like instrument, this page provides the theoretical geometric foundation, defining similarity of figures and beginning a formal proof that any shape can be replicated at a specific ratio using a fixed pivot point and proportional segments.</meta>

( 13 )

...three-dimensionally, for the drawn shapes are three-dimensional if we imagine the marks, in their proximity to one another, drawing the surfaces of the shapes. This is because it is not impossible to imagine this arrangement in **sensible things** <term>al-maḥsūsāt</term> <note>Physical objects perceived by the senses.</note>, and it is even truer and more correct in **intelligible things** <term>al-ma'qūlāt</term> <note>Abstract concepts or geometric ideals understood by the mind.</note>.

In another way, shapes are called **similar** <term>mutashābihah</term> if, when one is drawn within the other and a certain point is assumed, the lines extending from that point to the boundaries of the shapes—whether lines or surfaces—are cut by the boundaries of the shapes in that same ratio.

[14] Having established this, we shall demonstrate that it is possible for us to find a shape similar to any given shape, having a known ratio to it. First, we demonstrate this regarding **surfaces** <term>al-suṭūḥ</term>.

Let us assume a certain line, which is line $\overline{A B}$, fixed at point $\overline{A}$ and moving over a surface upon which there are two marks, namely our marks $\overline{B}$ and $\overline{H}$ <note>The Arabic letter Ḥā (ح) is used here as a geometric point, which I have rendered as H.</note>, which pass along the lines. Let mark B draw a line [forming the shape] $\overline{B G D E Z}$ on the surface, and let mark H draw a line $\overline{H T K L M}$. 

We say that the two shapes $\overline{B G D E Z}$ and $\overline{H T K L M}$ are similar. The proof of this is that we draw within $\overline{B G D E Z}$ a **rectilinear shape** <term>shakl mustaqīm al-khuṭūṭ</term> <note>A polygon composed of straight line segments.</note>, which is shape $\overline{B G D E Z}$. We connect point $\overline{A}$ to the points $\overline{B G D E Z}$, which are the lines we have already extended, and likewise we connect [to] $\overline{H T K L M}$. 

Because the lines $\overline{B A}$, $\overline{G A}$, $\overline{D A}$, $\overline{E A}$, and $\overline{Z A}$ have been divided in a similar proportion at points $\overline{H T K L M}$ according to what we assumed, then the rectilinear shape $\overline{B G D E Z}$ is similar...

[12] As for the method required for one who wishes to increase or decrease **regular figures** <term>al-ashkāl al-murattabah</term>—whether they be **plane surfaces** <term>suṭūḥiyyah</term> or **solids** <term>mujassamah</term>—according to a known ratio, we have already given an account of that. Figures are called **similar** <term>mutashābihah</term> if, when we draw certain shapes within one of the rectilinear figures, we are then able to draw shapes similar to them in the other. ۝

<meta>The previous translation (labeled page 13) discussed the broader application of geometric scaling to irregular shapes. This page, numbered 12 in the manuscript, appears to be the specific geometric proof that precedes those applications. It details a mechanical construction using a rotating ruler to find two mean proportionals between two given lines, a classic problem in ancient geometry often associated with doubling the cube.</meta>

# 12

For the two given lines $AB$ and $BC$, let one of them be perpendicular to the other. These are the two lines for which we wish to find two **mean proportionals** <term>mutawassiṭayn mutanāsibayn</term> <note>Intermediate values in a geometric sequence, such that $A/X = X/Y = Y/B$. Finding two means was a famous challenge in Greek and Islamic geometry.</note> between them. We complete the rectangle $ABCD$ and extend the lines $DC$ and $DA$. We connect $BD$ and $CA$. We then place a **ruler** <term>qānūn</term> <note>original: "qānūn" — Here referring to a physical measuring instrument or a mechanical straightedge used to find intersections.</note> upon the mark $B$ that intersects the lines $DA$ and $AZ$. One rotates it until the line extending from point $H$ to the intersection $GE$ is equal to the line extending from point $H$ to the intersection $AZ$. 

Let the position of the ruler be $EBZ$, where the lines $EH$ and $HZ$ are equal. I say that the lines $AZ$ and $GE$ are the two mean proportionals between the lines $AB$ and $BC$; the first is $AB$, the second is $AZ$, the third is $GE$, and the fourth is $CB$.

The **proof** <term>burhān</term> of this is as follows: since the square $ABCD$ is a **parallelogram** <term>mutawāzī al-aḍlāʿ</term> with **right angles** <term>qāʾim al-zawāyā</term> <note>A rectangle.</note>, the four lines $DH$, $HA$, $HB$, and $HC$ are all equal. Because the line $HD$ is equal to line $HA$, and the line $HZ$ has been drawn, then the **product** <term>maḍrūb</term> <note>In this context, the area of a rectangle formed by these segments.</note> of $DZ$ and $ZA$ plus the square of $AH$ is equal to the square of $HZ$. 

Likewise, the product of $DE$ and $EG$ plus the square of $HC$ is equal to the square of $HE$. Since the lines $EH$ and $HZ$ are equal, the product of $DZ$ and $ZA$ plus the square of $AH$ is equal to the product of $DE$ and $EG$ plus the square of $HC$. Furthermore, the square of $HC$ is equal to the square of $AH$. Therefore, the remaining product $DE$ times $EG$ is equal to the remaining product $DZ$ times $ZA$. Thus, line $DE$ is to $EZ$ as line $ZA$ is to $GE$, and line $DE$ is to $DZ$ as line $BA$ is to $AZ$, and line $GE$...

...a single ratio. First, regarding **simple shapes** <term>al-ashkāl al-basīṭah</term> <note>In this context, 2D geometric figures such as squares or triangles.</note>, let us assume a line of a known type. We wish to find another line such that the two figures drawn on these two similar lines have a ratio to one another equal to the **given ratio** <term>al-nisbah al-maʿlūmah</term>. 

Let there be a given ratio between the known line and another line. Let us assume a third line between these two known lines; this is the required line. This is because when three lines are in proportion <note>For example, a sequence like 2, 4, 8, where 4 is the "mean."</note>, the ratio of the first to the third is the same as the ratio of the figure of the first to the similar figure of the second drawn in similarity.

[10] But here we wish to find another line such that the **solid shapes** <term>al-ashkāl al-mujassamah</term> <note>Three-dimensional figures such as cubes or spheres.</note>—which are drawn similarly from two similar lines—have a given ratio to one another. Let there be a line that has a certain known ratio to another line. We assume two other lines between these two lines in **continuous proportion** <term>al-nisbah al-muttaṣilah</term> <note>A geometric progression where the ratio between each consecutive pair is equal ($a:b = b:c = c:d$).</note>. 

If we do this, we have found what we sought. This is because when four lines are in continuous proportion, the ratio of the first to the fourth is the same as the ratio of the solid figure of the first line to the similar solid shape of the second line drawn in similarity.

[11] As for how we extract **two proportional lines** <term>khaṭṭayn mutanāsibayn</term> <note>These are the two intermediate values needed to solve the problem of scaling a volume (like "doubling the cube").</note> between two given lines, we shall demonstrate this using an **instrument** <note>original: "āla" — Referring to a mechanical device rather than a standard compass-and-straightedge construction.</note>. We do not strictly require this for the solid body itself, but we provide it here as it is much easier in practice. Let...

...its position will be¹ on line EZ. For the moving mark A on line AB will be, at that same time, on line EZ. Thus, there is a single ratio: the ratio of line AC to line AB—that is, to line CD—is as the ratio of line AE to the line extending from mark E to the mark moving upon it. 

The ratio of line AC to line CD is the same as the ratio of AE to EH. Therefore, the mark moving along line AB will arrive at point H on line AD, which is the **diagonal** <term>al-quṭr</term>. 

In the same manner, it is demonstrated that the mark passing along line AB is always also passing along line AD. During that time, it moves along each of the lines AB and AD at different rates. Thus, the mark, moving with **uniform motion** <term>al-ḥarakah al-muʿtadilah</term> <note>Constant speed where equal distances are covered in equal times.</note> in a single span of time, passes over two unequal lines. 

However, as we have stated, the motion of the mark on line AB is **simple** <term>mabsūṭah</term> <note>Direct or uncomposed linear motion.</note>, while its motion along the diagonal AD is **composed** <term>muʾallafah</term> from the motion of AB along the lines AC and BD, and from the motion of A along line AB. Thus, mark A, in one and the same time and with uniform motion, passes over two unequal lines. And that is what we intended to demonstrate.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "Aristotle's Wheel" paradox, where two concentric circles of different sizes appear to travel the same distance. The arc of the **large circle** <term>al-dāʾirah al-ʿuẓmā</term> is equal to the arc of the **small circle** <term>al-dāʾirah al-ṣughrā</term>. We state that the arc of the small circle does not simply roll along line G-G alone; rather, the small circle travels the same path as the large circle simultaneously. Thus, for the small circle, a motion occurs that is equal in speed to the motion of the large circle by means of two motions. 

For if we imagine the large circle rolling while the small circle is not rolling, but remains fixed on mark G alone, it would—in that same span of time—pass along line G-G. Therefore, the center A in that time passes along line A-A, which is equal to lines B-B and G-G. Thus, the rolling rotation of the small circle is not what performs the movement. We see that the center, which does not roll at all, traverses that same distance through the motion by which the large circle moves. <note>This explains the "Wheel Paradox": the smaller circle covers a distance equal to the larger one's circumference not just by rolling, but by being "carried" along by the larger circle's rotation.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Aristotle's Wheel" paradox. [٨] ...at an equal speed. Since the circles are fixed <note>Meaning they are joined together as a single solid unit.</note> and move around a single center, let us imagine two circles fixed on a single center, which is center A. Let there be a line tangent to the **large circle** <term>al-dāʾirah al-ʿuẓmā</term>, which is line B-B. Let us connect points A and B; thus, line A-B is perpendicular to line B-B.

Line B-B is parallel to line G-G. Therefore, line G-G is tangent to the **small circle** <term>al-dāʾirah al-ṣughrā</term>. Furthermore, let us extend through point A a line parallel to these lines, which is line A-A.

If we imagine the large circle rolling <note>original: "mudahrajah," referring to a rolling motion without slipping.</note> along line B-B, then the small circle rolls along line G-G. If the large circle has completed one full rotation, it appears to us that the small circle has also completed one full rotation. Consequently, the position of the circles is the same as the position of the circles centered at A, and the position of line A-B is the same as the previous position of line A-B. <insert>Thus <note>The manuscript originally wrote "faladhālika" (therefore), but a correction suggests "fadhālika" (that).</note></insert> Therefore, line B-B is equal to line G-G.

Line B-B is the line along which the large circle rolls when it completes one rotation, and line G-G is the line upon which the small circle revolves <note>original: "yaltaffu," literally "to wrap around" or "to wind."</note> when it completes one rotation. Thus, the motion of the small circle is of **equal speed** <term>musāwiyat al-surʿah</term> to the motion of the large circle, because line B-B is equal to line G-G.

For things that traverse equal distances in equal times, their motion is of equal speed. Perhaps this statement might be thought impossible, because it is not possible for—

<meta>This page continues the geometric exploration of concentric circles and their relative speeds. While the previous page focused on the "Aristotle's Wheel" paradox—where circles of different sizes appear to trace the same linear distance—this page analyzes how the direction and speed of motion change depending on the axis and the observer's frame of reference.</meta>

...the two circles; this is demonstrated in all circles that come in pairs. ❦

One may observe that motion is sometimes identical and sometimes opposite. This occurs not only between two or more circles, but even within a single circle. A single mark might be seen moving in one direction at one time, and in the opposite direction at another. For when that moving mark begins its motion from a certain point, it continues to move in one direction until it has traversed an arc of a semi-circle. However, once it passes the arc of the second semi-circle, it moves in a direction opposite to that first motion <note>This refers to the "return" motion of a point on a rotating circle relative to a fixed horizontal line.</note>. ❦

[6] Furthermore, large circles <term>al-dawāʾir al-ʿuẓām</term> are not always faster in motion than small circles <term>al-dawāʾir al-ṣughār</term>. On the contrary, small circles can sometimes be faster than large ones. If circles are fixed upon a single center, then the large circles move faster than the small ones <note>Because the larger circumference must cover more ground in the same amount of time.</note>. But if the circles are separated and yet part of a single body—meaning they are <margin>not</margin> <note>The word "ghayr" (not/other than) was added in the margin to clarify that the circles do not share a single axis of rotation.</note> on the same axis, as can occur in the large wheels of the celestial sphere <term>al-falak</term>—then the small circles move faster than the large ones. Because their motion is linked as one, and they move within the same span of time, the small circle must complete many rotations by the time the large circle completes just one. Therefore, the small circle becomes the faster in motion. ❦

[7] It is also possible for the motion of the small circle and the large circle to be...

Furthermore, in the same span of time <note>The word "in" was omitted in the original manuscript but is necessary for the sense of the sentence.</note> in which point G traverses arc **G-T-H**, point H traverses arc **E-H-D-K-H**—sometimes moving in the same direction as G, and sometimes in the opposite direction. [5] If we imagine a circle positioned so that it touches the circle centered at point B at point K, we can demonstrate that what we stated regarding the first circle also applies to a third circle. This is because if the first circle moves in a direction opposite to the second circle, and the second circle moves in a direction opposite to the third circle, then the motion of the first circle must be identical to the motion of the third circle <note>This describes a chain of three touching circles: Circle 1 turns clockwise, Circle 2 (touching it) turns counter-clockwise, and Circle 3 (touching Circle 2) turns clockwise again, matching Circle 1.</note>.

...arc **Z-G** to **H-D** are equal to **G-D**. This should be imagined as occurring on the arcs **H-T-D-K** and on the arcs **T-A-L-K**. Furthermore, we state that it is possible for motion to occur in a single direction. We say that the two points **D** and **E** move in a single direction because point **E** moves along arc **H-Z-G** while point **D** moves along arc **D-K-T**. Each of the two points **Z** and **K** are equal, and their proximity to them is equal. 

This specific type of motion is called **opposite** <term>mutadadda</term>. Therefore, the terms "opposite" and "identical" are relative <note>The author uses the philosophical term "relative" (al-mudaaf) to indicate that these directions are not absolute but depend on the relationship between the two moving points.</note>. It is necessary to distinguish in every movement which motion is identical and which is opposite. This should be imagined within the context of equal circles.

As for unequal circles, the following applies:

[4] Let the circles be unequal, and let their centers be located at points **A** and **B**. Let the larger of the two circles be the one centered at point **A**. In these circles, the specific arrangement found in equal circles is not maintained. 

Let us assume two points at a specific distance from point **E**. To illustrate this, let us make line **Z-G-E** double the diameter **Q-D** <note>original: "qutr Q-D"</note>. Consequently, arc **H-Z-G** will be double the length of arc **H-D**, for Archimedes <note>This likely refers to Archimedes' works on circular ratios or spirals, which were widely studied by medieval scholars to understand the relationship between diameters and arc lengths.</note> has already demonstrated this. 

Thus, in the span of time in which point **E** traverses arc **H-Z** while moving toward the direction of **G**, point **D** traverses arc **H-D** while moving in an opposite direction. Furthermore, in the time it takes for point **E** to finish its path from **Z** and traverse arc <gloss>point</gloss> **Z-G**, point **D** finishes its path from **D** and traverses arc **D-K** <gloss></gloss>.

<meta>The previous page established the geometry of relative motion in circles and gear-like systems. This page transitions from theoretical geometry to a specific mechanical application: a gear train involving multiple axes and pulleys used to lift a weight.</meta>

### On the Application of Gear Trains

As for what might arise from the **difficulty of the wheels** <gloss>1</gloss> <note>original: "عسر الفلك" (*'usr al-falak*). This likely refers to mechanical resistance or friction in the rotation of the celestial-like spheres or gears.</note>, it has become clear from what we have described that when the **mover** <term>al-muharrik</term> <note>The primary source of motion, such as a hand-crank or a falling weight.</note> rotates pulley **T-T**, the axis **Kh-Dh** rotates. Through its rotation, pulley **R-Sh** rotates, which in turn rotates axis **Y-S**. Consequently, pulley **N-Q** rotates, as does pulley **S-A** along with it. This causes axis **K-L** to rotate, which turns pulley **M-N** and pulley **H-T**. Finally, this causes axis **H-Z** to rotate, so that the **cables** <term>al-qulus</term> <note>Heavy ropes or cords used in ancient machinery.</note> wind around the axis and the **weight** is lifted. This is what we intended to demonstrate. <gloss>End of section. <gloss>2</gloss></gloss> <margin>It is necessary that the axis **Dh-Kh** be extended to point **D**, and a perpendicular line **D-Z** be erected upon it, equal to the radius of pulley **T-T** or greater. And God knows best.</margin>

This was written based on an educated guess that it should be thus: because stationary circles <note>The author refers to gears or pulleys fixed to a shaft.</note> on a single axis always move in one direction—the direction in which the axis moves. However, circles that are on two different axes and are interlocked with one another by **teeth** <term>dandanjat</term> <note>From the Persian *dandānah*; refers to the teeth of a gear wheel.</note> move in two different directions; one moves toward the right and the other toward the left. If the two circles are equal, one rotation to the right perfectly matches one rotation to the left. If they...

<summary>This page describes the mechanical transmission of force through a series of interlocking gears and pulleys to lift a heavy weight. --- Page 325 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of a mechanical gear train. The previous page established the sequence of shafts and pulleys; here, the author provides the mathematical ratios and force calculations required to lift a heavy load using a limited input force, referencing the "Five Powers" (mechanical advantages) of classical mechanics.</meta>

...through a hole in wall **A-B** so that it winds around axis **A-Z**, pulley **H-T** rotates. By the winding of the **cables** <term>al-qulus</term> <note>original: "القلوس" (*al-qulūs*). Large, heavy-duty ropes used in ancient lifting machines.</note>, the load is moved. To move pulley **H-T**, a force of two hundred **qintars** <term>qintar</term> <note>original: "قنطار" (*qinṭār*). A traditional unit of weight; the author uses it here as a unit of measure for both the weight of the load and the effort required to move it.</note> is required, because the diameter of the pulley is five times the diameter of the axis, according to what we have assumed. 

This has been demonstrated in the *Proofs of the Five Powers* <note>original: "براهين الخمس قوى" (*Barāhīn al-Khams Quwā*). This refers to the five basic mechanical machines—the lever, pulley, wheel and axle, wedge, and screw—formulated by Hero of Alexandria and later translated into Arabic.</note>. However, we do not possess a force of two hundred **qintars**; therefore, the pulley will not move. 

So, let us establish another axis parallel to axis **A-Z**, which is axis **K-L**. Let there be a fixed toothed wheel upon it, which is pulley **M-N**. Let pulley **H-T** also be toothed, so that it interlocks with the teeth of pulley **M-N**. Let there be another fixed pulley on axis **K-L**, which is **S-A**, whose diameter is five times the diameter of **M-N**. Thus, the force required to move the weight via pulley **S-A** is reduced to forty **qintars**, because one-fifth of two hundred **qintars** is forty **qintars**.

Furthermore, we mount upon pulley **S-A** another pulley, which is pulley **F-Q**, fixed on another axis, axis **Y-S**. Let there be another pulley fixed on this axis whose diameter is five times the diameter of pulley **F-Q** fixed on axis **Y-S**, which is pulley **R-Sh**. The force required to move the weight at the point of **R-Sh** would then be eight **qintars**. 

However, the force available to us is only five **qintars**. Therefore, let us mount another toothed wheel, which is pulley **T-T**, and let its diameter be twice the diameter of pulley **R-Sh**. Pulley **T-T** would then require a force of four **qintars**. Since the force available to us is five **qintars**, there is a surplus of one **qintar** in this force to ensure the movement. <note>The "surplus" acts as a margin of safety to overcome friction and ensure the machine operates smoothly despite the calculated resistance.</note>

<summary>This page provides a mathematical breakdown of a gear train's mechanical advantage, showing how a 200-qintar load can be moved by a 5-qintar force through a series of reduction gears. The author calculates specific ratios for four different axes to achieve the necessary force reduction.</summary>
<keywords>mechanical advantage, gear train, Five Powers, qintar, reduction ratio, pulleys, toothed wheels</keywords>

It identifies the translator as the famous Qusta ibn Luqa and the patron as a member of the Abbasid caliphal family.</meta>
