# The First Treatise of the Book of Hero on the Lifting of Heavy Objects
## Ordered to be brought forth from the Greek language into the Arabic language by Abu al-Abbas Ahmad ibn al-Mu'tasim, and its translation was undertaken by Qusta ibn Luqa al-Ba‘labakki <note>Qusta ibn Luqa (820–912 AD) was a prominent Melkite Christian physician and translator in Baghdad, known for his proficiency in Greek, Syriac, and Arabic.</note>

[3] We wish to move a known weight by a known force using an assembly of toothed wheels <term>gears</term> <note>original: "فلك ذات اسنان" (*falak dhāt asnān*). Literally "disks possessing teeth," the standard term for gears in early Arabic mechanics.</note> within a fixed frame shaped like a box. Within its long parallel walls, let there be rotating axes <term>shafts</term>, and let the distance between them be such that the teeth of one shaft interlock with the teeth of the other, as we shall demonstrate. 

So, let this shape be box **A-B-C-D**, and let there be an axis placed within it that moves smoothly, which is axis **E-Z**. Let there be a toothed wheel fixed upon it, which is pulley **H-T**. For example, let its diameter be five times the diameter of axis **E-Z**. 

Now, let our total weight be, for instance, one thousand **qintars** <term>qintar</term> <note>original: "قنطار" (*qinṭār*). A heavy unit of weight used throughout the Mediterranean and Middle East, varying by region but here representing a standard large load.</note> that we wish to pull, and the moving force be five **qintars**. If the person moving it—or even a boy—is capable of moving five **qintars** by himself without a mechanical device <term>ingenious machine</term> <note>original: "حيلة" (*ḥīla*). This term refers to a mechanical contrivance; the study of mechanics was known in Arabic as *'ilm al-hiyal* or "the science of ingenious devices."</note>, then [we can move the load] because we have introduced the limited cables <note>original: "الفلوس" (*al-fulūs*). This appears to be a scribal error for *al-qulūs* (القلوس), meaning thick ropes or cables, consistent with the terminology used on the previous page.</note> into the load...

<summary>This page introduces the Arabic translation of Hero of Alexandria’s Mechanics, commissioned by the Abbasid elite. It begins a technical description of a gear-reduction machine designed to allow a single person to move a 1,000-qintar load using only 5 qintars of force.</summary>

<keywords>Hero of Alexandria, Qusta ibn Luqa, gear train, mechanical advantage, qintar, ingenious devices, Abbasid translation movement</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the official title page for the 1894 printed edition of Hero of Alexandria’s mechanical treatise. While the previous page provided the traditional Arabic incipit (opening) found within the medieval manuscript itself, this page identifies the modern editor and the publication details in Paris, bridging the gap between the 9th-century translation and 19th-century European scholarship.</meta>

# The Book of Heron
## On the Lifting of Heavy Objects <note>This work is widely known in the history of science as Heron's *Mechanics*. While the original Greek text was largely lost, the work survived through this Arabic translation.</note>

->Translated from the Greek language<-

->Into the Arabic language<-

->By Qusta ibn Luqa al-Ba‘labakki<- <note>Qusta ibn Luqa (820–912 AD) was a prominent physician and translator in Baghdad who played a vital role in preserving Greek scientific knowledge for the Arabic-speaking world.</note>

->The editing and correction was undertaken by<-

->The servant in need of his Lord’s mercy<- <note>This is a traditional pious humble formula used by authors and editors in the Islamic world, even appearing here in a modern academic context.</note>

->Baron Carra de Vaux<- <note>original: "البيرون قَرَّة دؤُو" (*al-Bīrūn Qarra du-Vu*). Bernard Carra de Vaux (1867–1953) was a French scholar of Middle Eastern studies who specialized in the history of Islamic science and philosophy.</note>
